The web had nothing: no client plugin, no dependency, no button, no dialog section, no mock routes, no tests, which is what GAPS #12 and the passkey clause of GAPS #94 recorded. Two facts shaped the closure: the e2e stack proxies real HTTP (a Next rewrite to the mock Hono in `test/servers.ts`) and every Playwright project runs on Chrome, so a CDP virtual authenticator produces genuine WebAuthn answers that flow through the client library into the mock; and jsdom has no `navigator.credentials`, so unit tests stub it. The workspace's first-load budget (350 KB gzipped, GAPS #19) argued against putting the WebAuthn library on the main client.
**Decision.** (1) `apps/web/src/lib/auth/passkey.ts` holds a second Better Auth client with `passkeyClient()`, created on first use behind a dynamic import, so the WebAuthn library rides in its own chunk; the main client is unchanged. (2) The sign-in screen offers "Sign in with a passkey" only where the browser has `PublicKeyCredential`; success finishes the sign-in as a password does; a refusal is one sentence (ADR-078's TWO_FACTOR_REQUIRED names the password and the code, a closed prompt says so, no passkey on this device points at Security). (3) The Security dialog gains a Passkeys section: the list from the plugin (name, added date), Add a passkey with a name (default "This device"), inline Rename, Remove behind an inline confirm with a way back, a note when the browser cannot create one; its copy says a passkey never signs in an account with two-factor on. The plugin adds a passkey only on a session younger than a day (its fresh-session rule, the app's default fresh age); an older session is told to sign in again. The Create account tab does not offer passkey sign-in. (4) The mock serves the seven endpoints with the plugin's shapes for `rp.id` localhost, records the credential id the browser reports at registration and looks it up at sign-in (no attestation or signature check, the fidelity of its fixed TOTP code), and refuses a 2FA account like the API. (5) Tests: web unit (a stubbed `navigator.credentials`: add, rename, a closed prompt, remove, the unsupported note; the sign-in button hidden without WebAuthn, signing in the credential's account, the 2FA refusal, the no-passkey sentence), api (the routes are mounted: register options carry HapieCoin on the web host, the list starts empty and needs a session, a made-up assertion is refused), e2e on the CDP virtual authenticator (add, rename, remove in Security; add, clear the cookies, sign back in with the key), run locally on 13 Sep 2026 on the chromium project, one worker, one spec at a time (both green; this laptop's memory allows nothing heavier), and again in the PR's CI.
**Consequences.** A trader on a trusted device signs in without the password; an account with the authenticator on keeps ADR-078's rule and is told so at the button. Left open (GAPS #105): the mock verifies no attestation or signature (a forged `response.id` signs in on the mock, never on the API); the seeded-user fixture cannot pre-register a passkey the virtual authenticator holds, so the e2e adds one first; no conditional UI / browser autofill for passkeys; no passkey count on `/v1/me` or the settings menu row; a 2FA account cannot use a passkey as its second factor (the plugin does not offer it); the Delta sign-in page and the phone pass do not show the button until tested there; on the real API the 2FA refusal of a passkey sign-in rests on ADR-078's allow-list default and is not exercised by a passkey test (the api test proves the routes are mounted and that an assertion without the challenge cookie is refused); the e2e ran locally one spec at a time and runs in CI. Tests: `dialogs.test.tsx` HC-SH-137, `AuthForms.test.tsx` HC-PB-069, `auth.test.ts` (api), `settings.spec.ts` / `auth.spec.ts` (e2e).

## ADR-090 · Feature freeze at main 933dcdc for the first launch; the backlog is the only intake; the feature guide and the readiness report are the launch record (2026-09-13)

**Context.** After the roadmap items 1–12, 17–20, 24–28, the PWA half of 23 and the gaps batch (GAPS #61, #90, #15, #94, #4, #35, #12) merged, the user asked to stop adding features, put everything pending in a backlog with full detail, assess how ready the product is, test it end to end, and document every feature at two levels with screenshots so it can be tested by hand and explained to others.

**Decision.** (1) The feature set is frozen at main `933dcdc` (PR #95). Roadmap items 21 (currency), 22 (languages), 23b (Capacitor) and 29 (zod/mini) move to `docs/BACKLOG.md` together with every open GAPS row in full and the 62 spec rows still on mock data; the backlog is the only intake after launch. (2) The launch record is `docs/GO-LIVE-READINESS.md`: what was verified and how (the serial gate, the full Playwright suites on the desktop, Pixel 7 and iPhone 14 profiles, with traces), what stays unverified until the real venue and real devices, the risks by severity, the defects found and fixed, and the decisions the user still owes for deployment. (3) The feature guide lives in `docs/guide/`: one page per area with a plain-language overview, a numbered walkthrough, screenshots from the Playwright runs, and a table of every traced feature generated from `spec/traceability.json`, so the guide cannot drift from the spec. (4) The Playwright suites are the executable form of the guide: `test:e2e:ui`, `test:e2e:headed` and `test:e2e:report` scripts let a non-developer watch every feature test run and replay it from the trace. (5) A launch defect is a GAPS row and a fix on a branch, never a backlog entry.

**Consequences.** New feature work needs a deliberate unfreeze: a roadmap line moved back from the backlog with an owner. The guide's tables regenerate from the spec and its screenshots from the visual and guide specs, so they cost nothing to keep current; the overviews are hand-written and are updated when an area changes. Rejected: a single generated document from all 1006 spec rows (unreadable), and screenshots taken by hand (they go stale and are not reproducible).

## ADR-091 · Short option legs carry a conservative margin estimate; the whole placement is refused before the first order; buys go out before sells (2026-09-15)

**Context.** GAPS #108: a four-leg condor placed live on 15 Sep 2026 had both short legs refused by Delta with `insufficient_margin` while both long legs filled, leaving a long-only position and a Retry banner. The preview's wallet rules compared the available balance with the worst-loss estimate and the net premium only (ADR-029 chose to show no invented pre-trade margin figure because Delta publishes no pre-trade margin call), so the batch passed; legs were also sent in leg order, so a naked short was the first order out. The trader's question was the right one: when the margin is not there, no leg should be sent.

**Decision.** (1) Delta's product record carries `initial_margin` (percent of the underlying) and `initial_margin_scaling_factor` (percent added per contract), and its ticker carries `spot_price`; the venue client keeps all three (`VenueProduct.initialMarginPct` / `initialMarginScalingFactor`, `getTicker` → mark + spot). (2) `shortOptionMarginUsd` in the venues package estimates a SOLD option's initial margin per contract as (pct + factor × contracts) / 100 × spot × contractValue + mark × contractValue, USD, the factor read in the same percent unit as the percent (Delta's explainer: Initial Margin% = Initial Margin%_MIN + Slope × size; the unit is not verified against a worked example, see GAPS #109). The scaling is applied from the first contract and no spread relief is assumed: the estimate errs toward refusing, and a false refusal costs a retry after a top-up while a false pass costs a half-filled strategy. Delta's exact formula was not obtainable verbatim from its public docs on 15 Sep 2026; the shape follows its margin explainer (initial margin percent of the index price plus the option mark for a short, growing with size). (3) `LivePreviewLeg.marginEstimate` (null for a buy, a future, or when the venue gave no parameters or spot) and `LivePreview.marginRequired` (Σ short estimates + the premium paid, null when nothing could be estimated). The preview refuses when `marginRequired` exceeds the available balance, after the premium rule, with the sentence "Available … is below the margin the exchange will hold for the short legs plus the premium paid (estimate …); nothing was sent". The place, batch (Trade All, which also sums the estimates against the one wallet and shows `LiveBatchPreview.marginRequired`), adjustment-batch and add-legs routes run the same preview first, so nothing reaches the exchange; when the venue priced one short and not another the preview refuses by name rather than present a partial figure as the whole; when nothing could be estimated the older rules alone decide. (4) `placeEntries` and `retryFailed` send buy legs before sell legs (a stable sort, leg order kept within each side), so a short is never the first order of a batch with nothing on the exchange to offset it. (5) The Trade Preview and the adjustment review show "Needed for shorts · est." beside "Margin in use", in red when above the wallet. This supersedes the "no invented pre-trade number" clause of ADR-029 for short option legs only; "Margin in use" stays the exchange's own figure.

**Consequences.** A trader with a matching long already on the exchange may be refused although Delta would accept; the sentence says "estimate" and the remedy is a top-up or fewer lots. When the venue lists no margin parameters the older rules alone decide, as before. The order of fills inside a batch changed (buys first), which the adjustment tests reflect. Verified: venues trading tests (estimate values, fake and real client parse), api live.test.ts HC-TR-192 (estimate, refusal, no order written, send order), web trading-live.test.tsx HC-TR-192 (figure, red state, button off).

## ADR-092 · The venue live orders go to is named on screen: a TESTNET tag whenever the trading host is not a real exchange host (2026-09-16)

**Context.** GAPS #111: ADR-025 let `DELTA_TRADING_REST_URL` point the private calls at the Delta India testnet while the chain, spot and IV came from the real public feed, and nothing on screen said so. On 16 Sep 2026 the user read a testnet fill (335.5 against a real market of 105-130 on a thin same-day expiry) as a bad real-money fill; every live trade placed from HapieCoin since 9 Sep had gone to the testnet. The original site carries an explicit TESTNET / PRODUCTION environment beside its exchange connection code; HapieCoin had only a URL.

**Decision.** (1) The API names the environment once, from the trading host: `tradingEnvOf(tradingUrl)` answers `production` only when the host is a real exchange host (`api.india.delta.exchange`, `api.delta.exchange`) that does not say testnet or demo; everything else (the Delta testnets, a local mock, a proxy) is `testnet`, so the label errs toward warning; `Config.deltaTradingEnv` / `deltaTradingHost`. The API test harness trades on a local mock host and reads testnet. (2) `GET /v1/credentials` answers `trading: { env, host }` beside the key list (`TradingEnv` in the schema package); the host is shown, never a key. (3) The web renders one `TradingEnvTag` (amber "TESTNET", the host in its title) wherever live money is implied: the Exchange chip, the Live tab's exchange chip, every live card beside its Live pill, the net positions header, the trade mode dialog, the Trade Preview (beside the exchange name, and its red note becomes "on the Delta Exchange TESTNET with play money … no real funds move") and the Trade All dialog. On the real exchange nothing renders: the tag is a warning, never decoration. (4) The API's startup log names the trading host and environment.

**Consequences.** A developer or tester can no longer mistake a testnet fill for a real one, and a production deployment that accidentally keeps a testnet URL shows the tag to every user. The web mock trades on the testnet by default (`state.tradingEnv`), like the dev API, and tests flip it to production to prove the tag disappears; the optimistic cache writers after connect / disconnect keep the environment so the tag shows the moment a key connects; the web parses the environment non-strictly so a newer API may add fields. Verified: api config.test.ts + credentials.test.ts (HC-SH-138), web widgets.test.tsx + trading-live.test.tsx (HC-SH-138), Playwright live-trading and /analyse visual.

## ADR-093 · Every listed strike by default: the main chain and the adjustment workbench open on the whole ladder (2026-09-20)

**Context.** GAPS #115. The adjustment workbench reused the Builder's compact strike picker, a fixed window of 12 strikes either side of ATM with no way to widen it. On 20 Sep 2026 the user adjusted a short strangle (short 92,000 call and short 66,000 put, spot 80,486, 27 Nov ladder 64,000 to 101,000): the window was 68,000 to 92,000, so the short put the position holds was not on the chain at all, nor any strike to roll it down to, nor any call above 92,000 to buy as a wing. The cheap far strikes a trader buys as insurance are exactly the hidden ones. The main chain had a ±6 / ±12 / All control but opened on ±12, and the user asked twice (17 and 20 Sep) why strikes were missing when the original site shows the whole ladder. A ladder is 30 to 50 rows; the main chain is virtualised and the workbench table is small enough not to need it.

**Decision.** (1) `DEFAULT_CHAIN_RANGE = 0` (every listed strike) in `apps/web/src/lib/chain/range.ts`; the ±6 and ±12 buttons stay. (2) The UI store keeps a `rangeDefault` marker beside the persisted `chainRange`, like `lotsDefault` (ADR-028). ±12 was the old default, so a saved 12 without the marker is not a choice and gets the new default once; ±6 and All can only have been chosen and are kept, and a marked value always sticks. "Once" is per unmarked write: a tab still running the previous bundle writes without the marker, so its 12 is replaced again on the next load, a transient around a deploy that `lotsDefault` shares. (3) `usePickerChain` takes the range and answers both the view (`rows`) and the whole ladder (`allRows`); the workbench owns its range (its own constant, All by default, a ±12 / All control and a strike count beside the cap line) and re-centres on the ATM row before paint whenever the expiry or the range changes; the quick fixes always read `allRows`, so a view click never changes which fixes exist or how they rank; the keyboard highlight is a row index, so it starts over on a new expiry or range (in the workbench and in the main chain) and the focused row is scrolled into view; the Builder's picker dialog keeps its compact ±12 (amended 2026-09-26, see below). (4) Strikes still come only from the venue's instrument list (ADR-006): the change shows more of the list, it never creates a strike.

**Consequences.** The quick fixes see the whole ladder, so a roll can find a strike beyond the old window. A trader whose browser held ±12 sees All once and may choose ±12 again; a saved ±6 is kept. The workbench table is not virtualised: fine for a Delta ladder of 30 to 60 rows, to be revisited if a venue with 100+ strikes becomes adjustable. The Builder's picker dialog still hides far strikes when building a new trade; left as it is until someone asks (recorded in GAPS #115). Verified: web unit tests (store default and re-apply-once, workbench HC-TR-193 with a held strike 30-odd rows from ATM), Playwright chromium (HC-WS-016 rewritten for the All default with a reload, HC-WS-108, the workbench flow with the ±12 / All control).

**Amendment (2026-09-26, GAPS #123).** The Builder's "Select Option from Chain" dialog now opens on every listed strike as well: on 26 Sep 2026 the user saw a 27 Nov ladder cut at 12 strikes either side of the money and read it as missing strikes, the same confusion decision (3) had left in place. The picker gets the workbench's ±12 / All control and strike count above its table, and its ATM row is centred once per view (expiry and range) as soon as both the ladder and the spot are known, so a spot tick that lands after the chain never re-scrolls under the trader's hand. `usePickerChain` defaults to `DEFAULT_CHAIN_RANGE` too, so no caller can fall back to the retired window. Verified: web unit test HC-TR-027 (row count above 25 by default, ±12 narrows to at most 25, All restores), e2e HC-TR-027 (the full recorded ladder, ATM row in the viewport on open, after ±12 → All and after an expiry change), control runs of both with the fix removed.

## ADR-094 · Repair ideas in the adjustment workbench: a diagnosis, a catalogue built from listed strikes, ordered by the trader's goal; before → after on one basis; a deliberate unfreeze of one item (2026-09-20)

**Context.** On 20 Sep 2026 the user adjusted a losing short strangle (short 92,000 call and short 66,000 put, spot 80,486, 27 Nov) and found no suggestion worth the name: three quick-fix buttons (ADR-044 decision 7) that ignored the position ("Roll strikes up" moved every leg one strike, "Hedge with a call" covered one side and one strike only), with their effect hidden in a tooltip, and nothing that offered the most useful repair for an unlimited-risk short strangle, buying both wings. The user also asked about "insurance": cheap far options on both ends. The feature set is frozen (ADR-090: new feature work needs a deliberate unfreeze with an owner); the user asked for this one, reviewed the plan and said go, so this ADR records the unfreeze of exactly this item. It stacks on ADR-093, which put the far strikes on the chain. A code review of the first build (20 Sep) found that the cards could say things the loaded draft did not do; decisions 2, 3 and 6 below are written after it.

**Decision.** (1) `apps/web/src/lib/adjust/repairs.ts`, pure functions, replaces `fixes.ts`. `diagnose` measures every short contract on the shown expiry from spot in percent and in expected moves (the engine's one-sigma figure), names the tested side (the nearest short) and says it is under pressure when it is in the money or less than one expected move away; twin rows on one contract (an earlier add books a second row) are ONE short; `diagnosisLine` puts that in one sentence, and before the spot price arrives it says it is waiting and no idea is offered. (2) `repairIdeas` builds drafts only from the whole listed ladder (ADR-006), never the view slice. For a position with something sold: cap the risk with wings chosen BY PRICE in three variants (the nearest strike whose mark is at most 10 %, 25 % or 50 % of the short leg's mark on that side: cheap, balanced, tight; past the end of the ladder the furthest listed strike, with a note that the budget was not met; sized by the short lots NO long of that kind answers, short lots minus long lots, because a long nearer the money caps the risk as well and a wing on a debit spread would only cost money; two budgets that land on the same strikes are one idea), roll the tested short two listed strikes away, roll the untested side two strikes in while it keeps half an expected move (or 2 % of spot) of room, roll out to the next expiry, roll out and away, halve the tested short, close the tested short (all rows of the contract together). For a long-only position: convert to a spread, roll to a cheaper strike, roll out. A quote at zero or missing is not a price: the idea skips that strike. An idea's closes are applied first and a replacement is never netted against a leg on its way out (a spread on adjacent strikes rolls whole); same-side orders on one contract merge (never the chain's toggle); an order that would meet the OTHER side of its own contract (a held long two strikes out, the far leg of a calendar, two legs landing on one strike of a coarser ladder) or carry a short onto or past its long makes the idea unavailable with the reason, never a silent flatten; on a roll out a short lands on its own strike or the next quoted one FURTHER out, never nearer spot, a long on the nearest quoted strike, at most one listed strike is skipped, and the card says when a strike moved; wings add to a held long. (3) Each idea is priced by the existing engine in the worker through `useDraftFigures` (no pricing in the UI, rule 4) and shows before → after max loss, max profit, break-evens, POP, net delta, cash now and the order count, plus one line on what it gives up. Before → after is on ONE basis, the whole trade from entry: the lots a change takes off go to the engine as offsetting pairs on their own contract (the held lots at entry plus the opposite side at the mark, `realisedLegs` in model.ts), worth exactly the locked-in result at every price and date and no delta, for the pane's working change, the kept plans and the ideas alike; the legs the trader sees (`afterLegs`: ticket, Legs and Greeks tabs) are unchanged. Without it a roll that bought back a losing short showed a HIGHER max profit than keeping it, and every idea that closed a losing leg out-ranked the wings. This corrects the ADR-044 convention for "after". (4) The trader orders the list by a goal: reduce max loss, collect credit, get neutral, widen break-evens; the default follows the diagnosis (an unlimited loss first, a tested side next, else the credit). Tags are facts: "defines your risk" on every idea that gives an unlimited loss a floor, and one leader per goal ("largest credit" only when cash is received); nothing is ever called best, and the row of goals reads "Order by". (5) Loading an idea replaces the working change through the existing `applyDraft`; Review, the margin gate, the typed LIVE word and the Mindful pause are untouched, so an idea never sends an order. A change the trader built by hand (neither an idea nor a kept plan) is not replaced silently: Load turns into "Replace my change?" and needs a second click. (6) The panel sits under the position ticket, where a wide workbench has room, so it never pushes the strike chain down. The ideas are built AND priced on a copy of the market that settles at most every two seconds, so a spot tick re-runs nothing; a change of structure (legs, expiry, a chain arriving) applies at once; the list re-orders only when the figures have been quiet for 0.4 s or the trader picks a goal, so a card does not move under the pointer while results arrive one by one.

**Consequences.** `QuickFixes.tsx` and `fixes.ts` are gone; HC-TR-154 is reworded in place (IDs are positional). The workbench's "after" figures (strip, payoff, plans) now include the result a close locks in, so they differ from the payoff the Analyse pane shows once the adjustment is booked (open legs only), as realised P&L always has. Not built, recorded in GAPS #117: a delta hedge with the perpetual (the workbench model holds option picks only), fees in "cash now", ideas across more than the shown expiry and the next, a held future as cover. An adjustment swaps one risk for another; the panel says so and shows the cost beside the benefit. Verified on 20 Sep 2026: repairs.test.ts (19 cases on a hand-made ladder), model.test.ts (the pairs are worth the locked-in result at six prices at expiry and at three prices on two dates before it), RepairIdeas.test.tsx (the settle rule on fake timers), workbench.test.tsx (the card order by goal, the Load question, HC-TR-193), five control runs that each broke one rule (zero quote, a short landing nearer spot, collisions, cover, the exit price) and failed exactly the matching test, the full web unit suite, Playwright analyse.spec and both phone projects, and a look at the panel at 1920 × 1000.

## ADR-095 · A delta hedge with the perpetual in the adjustment workbench, on two foundations: a held future is priced from its entry, and a futures leg is margined in the live preview (2026-09-21)

**Context.** The repair ideas (ADR-094) hold option picks only; GAPS #117 (b) recorded that a delta hedge with the perpetual was not built. The user asked for it on 20 Sep 2026, reviewed the plan and said go, which is the deliberate unfreeze of this one item under ADR-090. A futures leg already travels end to end (schema `kind: "future"`, strike "", expiry "PERP"; the adjust route, paper booking, realised P&L and the live executor are kind-agnostic; one lot of BTCUSD is 0.001 BTC, one Delta contract). Reading that path found two defects that would make any hedge misleading, so they are fixed first and on their own (PR C1); the hedge itself follows (PR C2).

**Decision.** (1) **A held future is priced from its entry.** `toPricingLegs` replaced every future's price with today's spot, so a future opened at 78,000 was worth exactly nothing at 80,000 and the whole payoff, the break-evens and the workbench's before → after were shifted by what it had earned or lost, while the leg table (which uses the entry) said otherwise. A future now takes the spot only in LIVE price mode, that is when a mark source is given (the Builder in live mode, the portfolio greeks), where the index stands in for the mark a perpetual does not have here (GAPS #14); without one it keeps its stored price exactly as an option does. (2) **A futures leg is margined, and moves no premium, in the live preview.** `planLegs` gave a future no margin estimate and counted its full notional as premium: a LONG future of 0.35 BTC read as a 28,000 USD debit, a short one as premium received, which LOWERED the requirement. `futuresMarginUsd` (packages/venues) estimates (pct + factor × contracts) / 100 × price × contract value × contracts from the product's margin parameters, with the future's mark or else the underlying spot as the price, the same unverified percent unit as ADR-091 and no relief assumed for the options it hedges. The figure is a FLOOR, not a conservative one: `initial_margin` is the product's minimum rate (its maximum leverage) and the account's leverage setting is not read, so an account trading the perpetual at a lower leverage is held to more (GAPS #120). The premium total covers option legs only, in the single preview and in the Trade All batch alike, and the notional and the margin of a future use one price (the mark, else the index). An ENTRY into a future the venue gave no margin parameters or price for is refused by name ("The margin for BTCUSD could not be estimated"), because a linear leg has no worst-loss figure to fall back on. That refusal lives in `preview()`, not in the planner: an adjustment re-plans its adds AFTER its exits have filled, and a ticker blip there must not strand the position behind a refusal that says nothing was sent (code review, 21 Sep); an exit (reduce-only) is never refused for margin. The margin sentence names "the short and futures legs" when a future is in the placement, and the dialogs read "Margin needed · est." instead of "Needed for shorts". (2a) **The workbench marks a future at the index.** Pricing a held future from its entry made two older gaps visible: lots ADDED to a held future were priced (and sent to the API) at the old entry, and a CLOSED future vanished from "after" without the result it locks in, while a paper close was booked at the entry, realising nothing. `markOf` now answers the spot index for a future (it has no mark of its own here, GAPS #14), so added lots are priced and booked at today's price, an exit is sent there, `realisedLegs` pairs a future like an option, and `cashflow` leaves futures out (a future moves no premium; it counted the notional before). (3) **The hedge (PR C2, built 21 Sep 2026).** The workbench draft may hold a futures pick (`PickKind = call | put | future`, strike "" and expiry "PERP" as the schema has them; the expiry, zero-DTE and premium rules ignore it). A repair idea "Hedge the delta with the future" (`hedgeDelta`, last in both catalogues) sizes it at |net delta| ÷ lot size in whole lots from the engine's delta of the position on the panel's settled market, sold when the delta is positive and bought when it is negative, unavailable within one lot of neutral or while the delta is unknown, fully neutral with the lots editable after loading (no half-hedge cards), and netted against a future the position already holds (the same side adds to that leg, the other side closes it first and only the rest opens new, so the idea never books two opposing rows). The ticket has a "Future" row with B / S for the perpetual at the chain's lots a click, under the chain's own rules (a second click takes it off, the other side flips it, on a held future it nets). It is priced at the spot index, which is also sent as its expected mark, so the server's mark band (5 % by default, against the perpetual's own ticker mark) applies to it like to any add. A change that adds a future goes at MARKET only: the review dialog disables "Limit at mark" and says why, because a limit at the reviewed mark would rest at the index, another instrument's price, and could sit unfilled while the pane already shows a neutral delta. The dialog's balance warning compares the margin needed when there is an estimate, never a future's notional (a 350-lot hedge is 28,000 USD of notional against a few hundred of margin). The hedge card never carries "defines your risk", even where the engine finds a floor (a naked short call hedged into a covered call): `tagsForIdea` removes it, the figures stay the engine's. A loaded hedge still reads as the hedge idea while the card re-sizes it with the delta (a loose signature for futures picks), so the other cards do not ask "Replace my change?" over nothing. The idea names and nets the perpetual on the strategy's own venue, and is unavailable where that venue lists none. The card says what a hedge is not: it has no floor, so it never earns "defines your risk"; delta moves, so it is a snapshot that needs re-hedging; funding payments are not modelled.

**Consequences.** Every payoff of a position that HOLDS a future changes: it now agrees with the leg table. Live previews with a future change: the margin figure includes it and no longer swings by its notional, and a test double or venue that registers a futures product without margin parameters now gets a refusal (the API fixture registers them where a test needs an estimate). Recorded in GAPS #120: the futures margin percent is unverified against Delta until a first real trade (as GAPS #109), the reduce-only path of a future has no API-level test yet, and funding is not modelled. Verified on 21 Sep 2026: legs.test.ts (3 cases), model.test.ts HC-TR-196 (a held future: added lots, the exit price, the locked-in pair at four prices, no premium), trading.test.ts HC-TR-196 (2 cases, zero is not an estimate), live.test.ts HC-TR-196 on a real Postgres (a put with a LONG future: 24.9 USD required where the old rule gave 802.95, refused without margin parameters, nothing reached the exchange; a call with a SHORT future: 19.95 where the old rule asked for nothing; an index-only ticker is still sized and margined), live-batch.test.ts HC-TR-196 (two long futures: 15.9 required, debit 0, where the old rule refused for a 1,590 USD "premium"), two control runs that each failed exactly the matching test (the old price rule: "expected 80000 to be 78000"; the notional as premium: "expected '810.9' to be '24.9'"), the full web unit suite (110 files, 774 tests) and the API strategies / live / rules / settlement tests (92). A code review of the first cut found the planner refusal, the batch route, the added-lots price and the vanishing locked-in result; all four are fixed here.

**Addendum, PR C2 (21 Sep 2026).** Verified: repairs.test.ts HC-TR-198 (sizing and side, another lot size, within one lot, no delta, netting against a held future on either side), model.test.ts HC-TR-197 (the pick, its price at the index, the API body, no premium, no expiry, the B / S rules, netting on a held future), analyse.spec.ts "HC-TR-197 / HC-TR-198 the delta hedge in the browser …" (the card sells the delta in lots to the lot, the delta after is under two lots, no "defines your risk" tag, the loaded pick, the ticket's B / S, Review and a paper apply that books three legs), a control run that halved the hedge and failed the unit tests ("expected 175 … 350") and the browser test (50.6 lots off), the full web unit suite (110 files, 780 tests after the review) and analyse.spec (33 passed). A code review of the first cut found the false balance warning, the limit order resting at the index, the unenforced "never defines your risk" and the ticket titles; all fixed here, with repairs.test.ts HC-TR-198 now five cases (twin rows, both sides, a dated future, the tag rule, the loose signature), a saved plan holding a future in model.test.ts and the card's lots pinned against the position's delta in workbench.test.tsx. Limits in GAPS #121.

## ADR-096 · The visual gate: every picture compared against a committed Linux baseline within 150 pixels, on a mock stack whose calendar is pinned (2026-09-28)

**Context.** GAPS #119 and the open half of GAPS #20. visual.spec.ts and guide.spec.ts saved 161 pictures per run and nothing compared them, so a layout break failed no test; the spec's visual gate ("screenshot diff of every route in both themes … any change above tolerance requires explicit approval in the pull request") had no implementation. Two things stood in the way. A baseline is per platform: this Windows laptop and the Linux CI runner render fonts differently, so one baseline cannot serve both. And the pictures were not reproducible: the fake gateway ticks spot and marks every 500 ms from a seeded generator, the served expiries move with the real day (GAPS #114), every time-to-expiry figure moves with the hour, the mock API stamps its data with the real clock, the mock analytics series are anchored at the process start minute, a "Leg added" toast fades on its own timer and the Next dev badge expands and collapses by itself. Two runs minutes apart differed in the chain, the payoff figures, the screener and the replay; two runs a day apart would have differed in every expiry label.

**Decision.** (1) The visual suite runs on its own mock stack under `apps/web/playwright.visual.config.ts` (`pnpm test:visual`, ports 3110 to 3112), and the default e2e config ignores it. (2) That stack's calendar is pinned: `test/servers.ts` takes `MOCK_CLOCK_AT` (the process clock starts at that instant and flows at real speed, through the same `Date` proxy as the unit tests' setup, so the mock API's stamps, the replay instants and the analytics series never depend on the real day or hour; a clock that stood still stalled the mock's per-minute windows, so the venue preview never answered), `MOCK_CALENDAR=recorded` (the fake gateway serves the recording as recorded instead of moved to the real day) and `MOCK_TICK_MS=0` (every price is the fixture's). Every page's clock is pinned to the same instant with Playwright's fixed clock (`e2e/shot.ts` preparePage), so the app drops the same expired expiry and prints the same days to expiry on any day. The instant is `VISUAL_NOW = 2026-09-08T10:00:00Z` (test/expiry-shift.ts): the morning after the recording, with the 11 Sep, 18 Sep, 25 Sep, 30 Oct and 27 Nov ladders live at 3, 10, 17, 52 and 80 days. (3) `shot()` replaces every `page.screenshot`: it still saves the capture under e2e/__screenshots__ (docs/guide embeds those files) and, with `E2E_PIXELS=1`, asserts `toHaveScreenshot` against `e2e/__baselines__/<name>-chromium-<platform>.png` with `maxDiffPixels 150` (and never more than 0.2 % of a picture: a ratio alone let about 1,800 pixels of a 1280 x 720 picture differ, more than a wrong digit or a missing icon), animations disabled, the caret hidden and no retries; a picture waits for the network to be idle and until no toast is on screen, the two texts the mock stamps to the minute are masked ("fired h:mm" in the alerts dialog, "Last read HH:MM" on the public trader page: the mock's clock flows), and the Next dev overlay (`<nextjs-portal>`, whose badge and "issue" toast pop up on their own; `devIndicators: false` hides the badge but not the toast) is hidden by a style added before each picture (after hydration: React owns the whole document and drops a style injected earlier); a picture is always taken from the left edge, because /analyse scrolls sideways by a varying amount at 1280 px (GAPS #125). (3a) The visual web server runs with `NEXT_DIST_DIR=.next-visual` (next.config.ts `distDir`): `next dev` locks its build folder and refuses a second server in the same app, which is why a Playwright run used to need the developer's `pnpm dev` stopped; with its own folder the visual suite runs beside it. Next rewrites the tracked next-env.d.ts for that folder, so the suite's global teardown puts it back. (4) The committed baseline is the CI runner's: a manual `baseline` job (workflow_dispatch) runs the suite with `--update-snapshots`, runs it a second time in compare mode to prove the pictures reproduce, and uploads the folder as an artifact; the PR that commits it shows every changed picture, which is the spec's "explicit approval". The PR job's visual step only captures until that baseline is committed; from the baseline commit on it runs with `E2E_PIXELS=1` and fails on any picture above tolerance, and its report with the expected, actual and diff images is uploaded on failure. A laptop run with the flag writes its own win32 pictures on the first pass (gitignored) and compares on the next, so a change can be proven pixel-identical here before CI. (5) The baseline job starts by hand (workflow_dispatch) or when a branch named `baseline/<anything>` is pushed, which works before the workflow is on the default branch; such a branch runs nothing else. The write step calls Playwright directly, because `pnpm test:visual -- --update-snapshots` hands Playwright a literal `--`, the flag is lost and every missing baseline then fails its test.

**Consequences.** A layout break anywhere on the 161 pictures is a red CI job with the diff attached, not a picture someone has to remember to look at. Refreshing the baseline is a deliberate act (run the job, commit the artifact, review the PR), never a side effect of a green run. About 24 MB of PNG join the repo (the captures already tracked are about 26 MB). A runner image update on ubuntu-latest can shift font rendering; small shifts sit inside the tolerance, a systematic one means one refresh through the job. The visual pictures now show September 2026 for good (VISUAL_NOW), which is what a baseline needs; the other e2e specs keep the real-day calendar of GAPS #114. One clock stays real, the Next server's: the footer year it renders changes two pictures on 1 January, inside the tolerance (noted in GAPS #124). Rejected: producing the baseline in the Playwright Docker image on the laptop (the Windows node_modules cannot be mounted into Linux, and a full install per run is slow and heavy on this machine), masking the volatile text picture by picture (the expiry labels alone appear in dozens of places), and a runtime freeze endpoint on the shared fake gateway (built first, dropped: a separate pinned stack needs no runtime state at all).

Verified on the laptop, 28 Sep 2026 (win32 pictures, gitignored): `playwright test -c playwright.visual.config.ts --update-snapshots` wrote the 161 pictures (34 tests passed) and, after the code review's fixes (the 150-pixel cap showed the tour card placing itself again after it opens, which its two pictures now wait for), two further compare runs passed 34 of 34 each, with a developer's `pnpm dev` running beside them and next-env.d.ts left clean; a control run that widened the backtest panel turned guide/analyse-backtest red (21,267 pixels, 3 %) and the restored panel passed; `tsc --noEmit` and `eslint .` clean. The Linux baseline is CI run 36540692400 of 29 Sep 2026, started by pushing the branch baseline/visual: the runner's Chromium wrote the 161 pictures (34 of 34 tests) and a second run compared against them (34 of 34); they were committed from the run's artifact and the PR job compares from that commit on

## ADR-097 · A Time exit before the nearest expiry settles, so a calendar closes whole; a deliberate unfreeze of one item under ADR-090 (2026-11-07)

**Context.** On 7 Nov 2026 the user held a paper put calendar (sold 2,180 P 07 Nov, bought 2,180 P 08 Nov, 100 lots each) with a target armed and asked how to make it close every leg when the near leg expires. Nothing did that: the expiry settler (ADR-059 §2.4, `apps/api/src/settlement.ts`) books only the legs whose settlement instant has passed, at intrinsic, and archives the strategy only when nothing stays open, so after 17:30 IST the far leg stayed open as a naked long put with the target still armed. The Time exit (ADR-059 §2.3, A4b) could approximate it with an exit at a typed instant, which the trader has to compute from the venue calendar every time, and a days-to-expiry rule at 0 fires at the same instant the settler acts. The feature set is frozen (ADR-090: new feature work needs a deliberate unfreeze with an owner); the user asked for this one, reviewed the plan and said go, so this ADR records the unfreeze of exactly this item.

**Decision.** (1) The Time exit gains a third trigger, `settle`: whole minutes before the nearest expiry settles (`SETTLE_LEAD_MINUTES`: 1 to 720, default 15). (2) The instant is fixed when the rule is armed: the arm route takes the nearest settlement of the strategy's open dated legs (`nearestSettlement`, the same function the days rule counts to) less the minutes and stores it as the rule's level; it refuses a strategy with no dated leg and an instant already passed. The engine judges the stored instant exactly like an exit at a time (`now >= level`), so a fire missed while the server was down happens on its first tick back, which may be after the settler booked the near leg, leaving only the far leg to close, the wanted outcome either way. (3) A fire goes through the existing path unchanged: every open filled leg at the mark of that tick, shorts first, paper books with no orders, the reason squared off, the other rules disarmed. (4) The dialog shows the nearest expiry and the instant on the trader's clock, and the card and Details read "exit all legs N min before expiry settles" with that instant. (5) The instant does not follow the legs. An adjustment that adds a nearer expiry leaves it where it was (re-arming recomputes it; the card and Details show the armed instant, the dialog shows what a re-arm would fix now). When the expiry it counted to is no longer held before the instant (the near leg rolled or closed by hand) the engine disarms the rule with the note "the expiry it counted to is no longer held; arm it again" rather than exit the new position at a moment that means nothing to it; from the instant on it fires, late if need be, with "late" in the note. (6) The `trigger` column is text (migration 0015), so no migration; the type-level enum lists the new value. (7) Not built: a per-account default that arms this on every multi-expiry strategy, and a verification on a real XAUT calendar (settles 16:00 UTC by the venue calendar); both in GAPS #130.

Rejected: a strategy flag the settler acts on. The settler prices at intrinsic and has no marks for a far leg, and a live far leg needs the order path the rules engine already owns; one fire path, one audit trail, one disarm.

**Consequences.** A calendar or diagonal can be told, in one tick, to close whole before its first expiry settles; the trader no longer computes the venue's settlement instant by hand. The lead is minutes, not seconds, because the engine ticks every few seconds and a live exit needs time to fill before the venue settles the near leg. Verified 7 Nov 2026: schema tests (`strategies.test.ts`), API `rules-engine.test.ts` HC-TR-199 (fires at the instant and closes both legs of a calendar with the far leg at its mark; fires late on the first tick back after the settler booked the near leg, closing the far leg alone; is disarmed when the near leg was closed by hand before the instant; a live calendar sends two market exits, short first; refuses a perpetual-only strategy and 0 minutes), web `rules.test.tsx` HC-TR-199 (the unit, the shown instant, the refusal out of range, the card line, the draft back, the no-dated-leg message); typecheck and lint green on schema, api and web. A code review of the first build (7 Nov) found the stale-instant exit on a rolled calendar and the route counting resting live entries; both are fixed above.

## ADR-098 · Research-backed playbooks: a year of chain history reconstructed from the venue's candles, hypotheses tested one at a time, proposals never placed; a deliberate unfreeze of roadmap items 30 and 31 under ADR-090 (2026-11-09)

**Context.** The deep research of 8 Nov 2026 (`docs/research/proven-strategies-crypto.md`, six note files, our own runs on Delta data) found one strategy family both proven in other markets and confirmed on Delta's own history (long/flat trend following on BTC with a 40 to 150 day lookback: Sharpe 1.12 against 0.73 for buy-and-hold, drawdown 59 % against 77 %, a plateau not a peak), three worth testing (30 to 45 DTE premium selling, defined-risk expiry-day selling in the 14:30 to 16:30 IST window, volatility-based sizing) and the rest negative or uncapturable after Delta's fees (hero-or-zero, naked daily strangles, mean reversion, funding signals, carry, altcoin momentum, on-chain and calendar signals). The user asked to build this in: the 48 structures stay as they are and each gets a tested plan. The blocker is data: `chain_eod` (ADR-077) starts in mid-September 2026, about two months. A read-only probe on 9 Nov 2026 showed that Delta's public candle history (`/v2/history/candles`) serves hourly `MARK:` candles for every option it ever listed, expired ones included, back more than a year: dailies, weeklies, monthlies and quarterlies on BTC (step 200, quarterlies 500 to 1,000) and ETH (step 10, weeklies 20), with strikes appearing in the candles on the day they were listed. The overfitting literature in the research (McLean-Pontiff, Harvey-Liu-Zhu, Bailey-López de Prado) says a search over 48 structures times conditions times exits on one year of data yields winners by luck.

**Decision.** (1) Roadmap items 30 (AI-1 radar only) and 31 (playbooks) return from the backlog with the user as owner: a deliberate unfreeze under ADR-090, in four PRs in this order: chain history (this PR), backtest rules (the real Delta fee rule closing GAPS #129, entry conditions, exit rules, a walk-forward split and a bounded parameter sweep with a plateau readout), playbook records (six to eight playbooks named in the research as data, a nightly record per playbook showing its losing years and trial count, hero-or-zero as the standing negative control) and proposals (detect-and-propose cards, one tap into paper trading, live only through the existing typed-LIVE route). (2) History reconstructed from candles lives in `chain_eod` beside the recorded rows, labelled `source = "candles"` (a new column; recorded rows say `snapshot`), with no mark IV, never on a day the snapshotter recorded and never on the expiry's own settlement day; the live recorder (`chain-eod.ts`) counts only recorded rows when deciding whether a day is done and replaces a reconstructed row for the same instrument with its own, so a day both wrote reads as `mixed`; the day's mark is the close of the hourly bar that ends at the settlement hour, else the open of the bar that starts at it (a product Delta listed at the hour itself). The job (`apps/api/src/chain-eod-history.ts`) runs leader-only (ADR-062) when `CHAIN_HISTORY_DAYS` is not 0 (default 365), walks every expiry date from today plus 100 days back to the horizon, newest first, finds the listed strikes by walking the strike grid over the spot range of the expiry's life (bounded by how long its at-the-money strikes have candles) and outward until eight consecutive strikes have none, spaces requests 270 ms apart with up to three attempts per request, writes a day only once its settlement bar has closed (today's rows wait until 13:00 UTC, so the live recorder's pass at 12:00 finds the day free), and records one `chain_eod_backfill` row per expiry so a restarted job resumes; a scan made on or after the expiry date stands for good, an open expiry is rescanned after a week, a past date that comes back without a strike or a symbol that fails three times is retried on the next run instead of being remembered, and a run that failed anywhere repeats after ten minutes instead of a day; BTC and ETH only (XAUT's step was not probed; Deribit offers no candles through `VenueRest`). The Backtest tab's coverage line says how many days are reconstructed and how many recorded live. (3) Playbooks are tested as named hypotheses from the research, never as a catalogue-wide search; every published record shows its trial count and its losing years; nothing proposes a trade without a record and nothing places one without the trader.

**Consequences.** The backtest reaches a year back once the backfill completes (on the order of 100,000 public requests at under four a second, several hours, once; afterwards a few thousand a day for the open expiries); the API does the work in the background and a restart resumes from the watermark. Reconstructed days carry marks only, so a modelled exit on such a day uses the IV the leg was entered at and the tab says the day is reconstructed. Every structure in the catalogue can be backtested over the longer history from day one; only tested-and-passed plans will be recommended, starting with the ones the research gives reasons to believe in. Rejected: a separate history table (the engine, replay and the coverage line would fork), fetching in the browser (megabytes per run), 5-minute candles (288 times the requests for no gain at the settlement hour), and scanning every structure against every condition and keeping the best (the research's own warning).

**Verified (9 Nov 2026).** Probe: `MARK:` 1h candles answered for expired BTC dailies, weeklies (2 Oct), monthlies (25 Sep), quarterlies (26 Jun, step 500) and ETH dailies and weeklies, and for a daily a year back (12 Nov 2025). Fidelity against the live record: on 8 Nov 2026 the 11:00 UTC bar's close differed from the 12:04 snapshot by 0.08 to 0.15 % on the 25 Dec quarterly and by 0.4 to 1.1 % on the 8 Nov daily (the spot moved in those four minutes). Tests: `apps/api` chain-eod-history.test.ts 14 passed (helpers, the database run, the live recorder replacing a reconstructed day, a failing symbol, the wide seed, the job starter), chain-eod / iv-snapshot / backtest route 11 passed, the whole api suite 424 passed; pricing backtest 9 passed; web backtest panel 3 passed; typecheck and lint green on venues, schema, pricing, api and web. Live run: the dev API (tsx watch) applied migration 0024 and ran the job against the real exchange while this was built; at the time of writing BTC had 117 expiries scanned (24 Oct 2026 to 17 Feb 2027), 7,013 rows on days the live record did not have (back to 31 Jul 2026), 4,237 requests; the Playwright assertion on the coverage line runs in PR CI (a second Next dev server cannot start beside the running one). A code review found three defects in the first build (today's rows blocking the live recorder, the three-strike seed missing coarse ladders, one failing symbol stalling the run for a day); all three are fixed above and tested.

## ADR-099 · The backtest charges the venue's published fee rule, takes entry conditions and exit rules judged on recorded marks, and says why every trade ended (2026-11-09)

**Context.** PR 2a of the playbooks programme (ADR-098). The ADR-077 engine entered every recorded day, held to expiry and charged nothing; the research of 8 Nov 2026 found that fees decide the sign of most daily structures and that the only strategies with evidence are conditional (a trend filter, an IV-rank band, a defined exit). GAPS #129 recorded that the seeded Delta fee defaults (0.05 % capped at 10 %) overstate option fees three to five times: the exchange's product list gives 0.01 % of notional capped at 3.5 % of the premium, 18 % GST, and the taker fee again at settlement. The two research notes disagree on whether a worthless expiry pays that settlement fee.

**Decision.** (1) One fee rule per venue, as fractions, is the single source for what a fill costs: `VenueCore.feeRule` in the venues port (Delta India: 0.01 % / 3.5 % / 0.05 % / 18 % / settlement fee on; Deribit: null, not read); `packages/pricing/src/fees.ts` (`fillFee`, `settlementFee`) only knows how to apply such a rule. The backtest charges it on every fill and on an in-the-money settlement, with the settlement value as the premium; a worthless expiry pays nothing (the reading chosen; if a real settlement ever shows a fee on a worthless expiry, the rule needs a second base, the notional fee uncapped, not a flag; GAPS #133). The broker profile that drives the paper and live previews is unchanged in this PR; GAPS #129 narrows to that half. (2) `runBacktest` takes `fees`, `conditions` (spot above or below the simple average of the last N recorded spots including today; today's front-expiry ATM IV ranked inside a band against the recorded ATM IVs of the previous 365 calendar days, judged only with at least twenty points; UTC weekdays) and `exits` (target as a share of the maximum profit or of the credit when unbounded; stop as a share of the maximum loss or of the premium when unbounded; a time stop at N days to the nearest leg's settlement). A condition without enough recorded history is a skipped entry, counted as "no signal" and shown, never a silent pass. Exit rules are judged once per recorded day on recorded marks only, from the day after entry until the nearest leg settles; a day where any leg has no recorded mark is not judged; model prices never trigger an exit. Every trade carries `grossPnl`, `fees`, net `pnl`, `exitReason` (settled, target, stop, time, open) and `ruleInert` (a requested target or stop had no base: a near-zero premium on an unbounded payoff, so it could never fire); the stats carry `grossTotal` and `totalFees`; the coverage carries `skipped.noSignal` and the exit counts including `inert`. The route hands the engine the whole recorded history: `from` bounds the entries, the coverage and the equity, and the earlier days stay as history for the conditions. With none of these set the output is the ADR-077 engine's to the bit. (3) The route reads them from the query (fees=venue by default, fees=none to compare; a venue without a rule refuses fees=venue with a 400 before any data is read; the exits in percent), and the Backtest tab's Rules row sets them: fees (fixed to none, the select disabled, on a venue without a rule), trend filter (20 / 50 / 100 / 200-day average, spot above or below), IV-rank band (low, middle, high), weekday chips, target (25 / 50 / 75 % of max profit), stop (50 / 100 / 200 % of max loss) and time stop (1 / 2 / 7 days before settlement, only the values below the minimum days to expiry). The coverage line says what the figures are net of, how the trades exited and how many had an inert rule; the empty state names the entry conditions when they are what skipped every day; the table shows each trade's fees and exit reason.

**Consequences.** Every structure in the catalogue can now be backtested net of fees with a plan around it, over the year of chain history from ADR-098. The IV-rank condition is blind on reconstructed days (they carry no IV, GAPS #132), so for now it effectively sees the live record. Exits are daily: an intraday touch of a target is not seen, and a stop fires at the next settlement-hour mark, so real stops will be both earlier and worse than this shows (GAPS #133). The walk-forward split and the bounded parameter sweep with its plateau score and trial count follow as PR 2b. Rejected: reworking the broker profile and its dialogs here (triples the surface for a fix that is one field); judging exits on model prices between recorded marks (would invent fills); a catalogue-wide sweep inside this PR (the research's own warning about trials).

## ADR-100 · Walk-forward periods and a bounded parameter sweep with a plateau reading: the only parameter search in the product, and it says how many trials it ran (2026-11-09)

**Context.** PR 2b of the playbooks programme (ADR-098). With fees, conditions and exits in the backtest (ADR-099) a trader can tune a plan until it looks good, which is exactly how the research's warning comes true: Bailey and López de Prado show that a few dozen configurations on a few years of data all but guarantee an in-sample winner that fails out of sample, and McLean and Pontiff measured the decay. The product needs two honesty tools next to the tuning: a way to see whether a record held up after the period it was fitted on, and a way to see whether a chosen parameter sits on a lucky peak or a broad plateau.

**Decision.** (1) Walk-forward: `runBacktest` takes `folds` (2 or 3) and returns the shown range's trades cut, in entry order, into that many contiguous periods of equal count (the later periods take the extra trade), each with the full stats shape computed from its own closed trades and a drawdown read from its own cumulative P&L netted per exit day as the headline is; the headline figures are unchanged. The tab's Split control names two periods in-sample and out-of-sample and states in one sentence how the second period compares with the first (both made money; the first did and the second did not; the first lost and the second made money; both lost), with the entry counts beside it because both halves are small samples. Nothing is fitted automatically, so a plain split is the honest version; anchored or rolling re-fitting is deferred (GAPS #134). (2) The sweep (`packages/pricing/src/sweep.ts`, `GET /v1/backtest/sweep`) runs one or two of the five rule parameters the Rules row exposes (average length, minimum days to expiry, target %, stop %, time stop) over a grid with a hard cap of 48 trials (at most eight values on the first axis and six on the second; every value must obey the single run's own field bounds and the list must increase, so grid neighbours are one step apart; all refused with a 400 before any data is read), applies every trial to the same fee rule, conditions and exits, and reports per trial the net total, trades, win rate, max drawdown, profit factor and a plateau reading: the share of its grid neighbours (one step either way along each axis) whose net total keeps the same sign and at least half its size. The result carries the trial count and the cap; the best cell is the highest net total among trials with a closed trade. The tab's Sweep view shows the grid over the Rules row's own option lists (so every loadable cell is a visible option; the time stop keeps only the values below the minimum days to expiry), the best cell ringed, a sentence with the trial count, the best cell's parameters and its plateau, and the warning that a lone peak is noise and a broad plateau is evidence; clicking a cell loads its parameters into the Rules row without re-running the sweep (the swept fields are pinned in the sweep's own query), and the previous grid stays while a changed axis runs. This is the only place the product searches parameters. (3) Deferred (GAPS #134): a strike-offset axis (needs template materialisation changes), rolling or anchored walk-forward re-fitting, Sharpe-type or annualised metrics (daily-settled option P&L is too lumpy for sixty-day windows), saved or shared sweeps, and the deflated Sharpe ratio proper.

**Consequences.** Every published playbook record in PR 3 can carry its in-sample and out-of-sample halves and the trial count behind its parameters, which ADR-098 requires. A sweep costs at most 48 engine runs over the recorded history (well under a second each) and is cached five minutes like the single run; nothing is persisted. The plateau reading is deliberately simple (same sign, half the size, grid neighbours only) so a trader can check it by eye on the grid. Rejected: an unbounded or user-sized grid (the cap is the point), a search that returns only the best cell (it would hide the trial count the research says to publish), and re-fitting inside the folds (there is no automatic fitting to re-run yet).

## ADR-101 · Playbooks as data with a stored record and an honest status: seven plans from the research, one of them a control that ships to fail (2026-11-09)

**Context.** PR 3 of the playbooks programme (ADR-098). The catalogue holds 48 structures and, since ADR-099 and ADR-100, the backtest can carry a plan around any of them and show its evidence. What the product lacked was the plan itself: a named structure with its entry conditions, exit rules, size, the reason it should work, the evidence it rests on, and a record of how it did on our own data. The research of 8 Nov 2026 named the candidates and their verdicts; `docs/research/strategy-playbooks.md` had sketched eight plans before the engine could run them.

**Decision.** (1) Playbooks are data in the pricing package (`packages/pricing/src/playbooks.ts`): id, name, a one-sentence summary, a rationale, the evidence with its source, a catalogue template name or an inline template, the engine's conditions and exits, the minimum days to expiry, a fixed size of 10 lots, the assets, thresholds, the number of parameter configurations looked at before these were fixed, and a control flag. Seven ship: trend with calls (Bull Call Spread above the 50-day average, 14+ DTE, target 50 %, stop 50 %, time stop 2), trend with puts (the mirror), premium harvest (Iron Condor at IV rank 50 to 100, 30+ DTE, target 50 %, stop 50 %, time stop 21), expiry-week credit spread (Bull Put Spread above the 50-day average, 3+ DTE, target 50 %, stop 50 %), pin butterfly (Long Call Butterfly, 1+ DTE, target 75 %), covered call (Covered Call, 30+ DTE, target 50 %, time stop 7), and hero-or-zero (an inline call four listed strikes out of the money, 1+ DTE, held) as the control. (2) A record is a stored fact about the data: `playbook_records` (migration 0025) holds one row per playbook, venue and asset with the engine's result over the whole recorded history, net of the venue's fee rule and with two folds, summarised as stats, the halves, the losing years by exit year, the exit counts, the trial count and a status. A leader job (`apps/api/src/playbooks.ts`, `PLAYBOOK_RECORDS_MS`, default six hours, once at start) recomputes a record whenever the recorded history changed, judged by the same stamp as the day cache (last recorded day plus the day count, so the candle backfill's older days and a day re-recorded live both count), one playbook's failure never stalling the rest; `GET /v1/playbooks` serves the venue's records (503 before the first). (3) Status rules: `pass` needs at least twenty closed trades, a positive net total and a positive out-of-sample half; `fail` has the trades but misses one of the two; `insufficient` lacks the trades; the control inverts to `control-ok` when it fails and `control-alarm` when it passes, and the job logs the alarm. Thresholds are stated on the card as first guesses. (4) The Playbooks tab (`PlaybooksPanel.tsx`, also in the palette) shows one card per playbook for the workspace asset: the plan in words, the summary, the rationale, the evidence with its source, the record (entries and closed trades, coverage with reconstructed days, net of fees with win rate and worst fall, in-sample against out-of-sample, losing years, the trial count), the status with its meaning, and two actions: Load into Builder (the Templates panel's own loader) and, for catalogue templates, Open in Backtest (a one-shot preset the Backtest tab applies to its template, size, a one-year range, the two-period split and the Rules row; the Rules row gained a 21-day time stop and an upper-half IV band so every playbook's rules are visible there; the control's inline template is not in the catalogue, so its card says its record is the backtest). A record carries whether it is net of the venue's fee rule or before fees (Deribit publishes none), and the card's basis says which. The copy says decision support, never a recommendation, and that a backtest is not a forecast.

**Consequences.** The first records on the dev stack (9 Nov 2026, BTC over 9 Nov 2025 to 9 Nov 2026 from the reconstructed history, 10 lots, net of the Delta rule) read: hero-or-zero `control-ok` (364 entries, net about -822, both halves negative); trend with puts `pass` (136 trades, net about +23, out-of-sample about +10); trend with calls `fail` (167 trades, net about -226 in a year BTC fell); covered call `fail` (325 trades, net about -2,922, the perpetual's losses in that year); expiry-week credit spread `fail` (168 trades, net about -67); pin butterfly `fail` (364 trades, net about -200); premium harvest `insufficient` (4 trades: the IV rank sat below 50 almost all year). ETH, with two months of recorded history, is `insufficient` or thin everywhere. That is the point of the thresholds: the record says what the data says, and a plan the research favoured over six years still has to earn its pass on the year we hold. Two research playbooks are not expressible yet and are not shipped: the calendar on a term-structure inversion and the butterfly centred on max pain need conditions the engine does not have; the 14:30 to 16:30 IST expiry-day window needs intraday data (GAPS #135). Proposals and notifications are PR 4; nothing here fires on its own, and Load into Builder reaches the live routes only through the existing Builder, typed-LIVE interlock and second factor. Rejected: computing records on request (seven engine runs over four hundred days per tab open), a per-user record (the record is about the data), and hiding the control (its job is to be seen failing).

## ADR-102 · Proposals: every playbook says whether it would enter today, from the recorded chain, into the Builder as paper and never placed (2026-11-10)
**Context.** PR 4 of the playbooks programme (ADR-098), the last one. The records (ADR-101) tell a trader how each plan did on our history; nothing yet told them what a plan would do today. The research and ADR-098 fixed the shape in advance: detect-and-propose, one tap into paper trading, live only through the existing typed-LIVE route, nothing placed by the system. The open questions were the data the detection reads, which playbooks may propose, how a proposal reaches a trader away from the screen, and how the one tap works without a second order path.
**Decision.** (1) A proposal is computed from the recorded settlement-hour chain, the same `EodDay` the records use, through the same entry logic: the backtest's entry step is extracted into `entryFor` (pricing) and `proposalFor` wraps it on the latest day, so a proposal is exactly the entry the record would have taken. There is no live-chain mirror in the browser (rejected: it would propose on a different basis than the record, disagree with the server row, and duplicate the entry logic). (2) The detector runs in the API inside the first venue's snapshotter after-hook (every few minutes, leader only), idempotent per playbook, venue, asset and day (`playbook_proposals`, unique index), so a proposal lands within minutes of the end-of-day write and never twice. (3) Every non-control playbook that has a record proposes, whatever its status: the card shows the status chip, and the tab stays honest about what each plan would do; hero-or-zero never proposes; nothing proposes without a record (ADR-098). (4) Marks are per user (`playbook_proposal_marks`): `taken` once opened in the Builder, `dismissed` to hide, and the Telegram delivery time; `GET /v1/proposals` joins them for the caller, `POST /v1/proposals/{id}/mark` sets them. (5) Telegram (through the ADR-057 bot) only for proposals whose record status is `pass`, only to users who linked a chat and switched `proposalsTelegram` on in settings (off by default), at most `PROPOSALS_TELEGRAM_CAP` a day per user (default 3; 0 off); a failed send is not marked and is retried on the next run; the message names the plan, the structure, the premium and the max loss, says a backtest is not a forecast, and links the app. (6) One tap: the proposal's exact legs (listed strikes, the plan's expiry and size) go into the Builder through the same `addLeg` the templates use, the trade flow opens with paper preselected, and the mark becomes `taken`; nothing is placed until the trader confirms in the flow, and live stays behind the typed word. (7) Without a day asked, the routes answer for the newest proposal day, which is the latest recorded chain (yesterday until the 12:00 UTC write), never the calendar day; an empty day is an empty list (200), not an error: most days no plan enters. Delivery likewise works from proposals detected in the last day, the marks keeping each from going out twice.
**Consequences.** Proposals appear once a day, after the venue's settlement-hour chain write (12:00 UTC on Delta India, 08:00 UTC on Deribit), not intraday; they stay listed as the newest day's until the next write replaces them; a trader who wants an intraday read uses the Backtest tab's rules on the live chain by hand (GAPS #139). The strip in the Playbooks tab lists today's proposals for the workspace asset above the record cards, with net premium, max profit, max loss (or unbounded), the record status and the warning copy; dismissed cards leave the strip and the count stays. Traceability HC-TR-207 (detector and table), HC-TR-208 (routes and marks), HC-TR-209 (Telegram delivery), HC-WS-120 (the strip and the one tap), HC-SH-141 (the opt-in setting and the cap). Migration 0026 adds the two tables and the `proposals_telegram` column. What is left out is GAPS #139.

## ADR-103 · A target can trail: reach the target, then exit on a fall from the best profit (2026-11-17)
**Context.** On 17 Nov 2026 a run of paper straddles and strangles closed at their fixed targets during a sudden move that went on in their favour; the trader asked for a target that follows the profit. Nothing of the kind existed: the rules engine (ADR-059 §2.3) closes at the first tick at or above the level. A survey of what traders already know (AlgoTest, Tradetron, Quantsapp, Algomojo, Dhan, Zerodha GTT; Sensibull has none) found one model everywhere: trail the whole strategy's P&L in the app, behind an activation level, with a floor that never moves down. No venue trails an options structure at the exchange (Deribit refuses trailing stops on options, Delta India trails a single contract only). The same survey shows the four-field form (activate, lock, step, trail) is the thing novices set wrong. The feature freeze (ADR-090) is lifted for this item at the user's request; owner: the user.
**Decision.** (1) Trailing is a mode of the Target rule, not a sixth kind: `trail` on the rule with a unit (`money` in USD, or `pct` of the best profit) and one value, the give-back. The target is the activation level. Two numbers in all; the step is continuous. A strategy keeps one target, fixed or trailing, chosen per strategy. (2) Below the target nothing happens. The first tick at or above it starts the trail and exits nothing. From then the rule keeps the best P&L seen and fires when the P&L falls to the best less the give-back; that exit level only rises. (3) A money give-back must be below the target (and at least a cent) and a percentage below 100, refused in the schema (so in the API and the mock alike) and in the dialog: the exit level is a profit from the moment the trail starts. (4) The best is stored on the rule row (`peak_pnl`) in whole cents rounded down, so it is never above the P&L that set it, and written only upwards (the update refuses a lower value), so a restart or another replica resumes from it; it stays on the row after the fire as the record of how far the profit ran. Saving the rules again keeps the best as long as the new target is not above it: the trader edited another rule, changed the give-back (the exit level moves at once), or a percentage target was re-priced on today's credit. A target raised above the best starts over and waits for the new level. The dialog says which of the two will happen before Arm, shows the exit level the save produces, and refuses one the P&L is already at or under. The strategy's realised total is read again beside the open legs on every pass (code review, 17 Nov 2026): the row from the pass's first query can be seconds old, and a trailing exit sits close under the current P&L. (5) Everything else is the existing fire: the same claim guard, the same exit path short legs first, paper without orders, the close reason `target`, the note naming the best and the exit level, mail and Telegram as chosen. The stop loss, leg stops, spot level and time exit are judged first, as before. (6) The Protect dialog adds one line inside the Target box, off by default, with a sentence of two worked exit levels; the card reads `target at +X, then trail Y` before the target and `trailing: best +X, exits at +Y` after; the fired chip reads `target hit · trailed from +X`. Rejected: the four-field lock-and-trail (more to get wrong for the same behaviour); a trail with no activation level (premium decay noise exits it at once); trailing at the exchange (not offered for options structures); a trail in the backtest's daily exit rules (judged once a day on settlement-hour marks it would describe a different thing than the 2 s engine).
**Consequences.** The exit is a market exit at the mark of the tick that saw the fall, so in a gap the realised P&L can be under the exit level, and after a fast reversal under the target itself; the fired note and `firedPnl` carry the P&L at the marks of that tick, and a live fill can differ from it (the adjustment row carries what was booked). The engine looks every `RULES_TICK_MS` (2 s): a best that lived between two ticks is never seen, and while the API is down the best does not rise and nothing fires (GAPS #82 as before). One more database write per trailing strategy only on ticks that set a new best of at least a cent, and one more read per valued strategy per pass (the realised total). A percentage of the best tightens in money terms when the best is small; money is the default unit for that reason. Traceability HC-TR-210 (schema, route, engine) and HC-TR-211 (dialog, card, chip, Details). Migration 0027 adds `trail_trigger`, `trail_value`, `peak_pnl` to `strategy_rules`, all nullable. Left out: GAPS #140.

## ADR-104 · A leg stop can hand the rest to a trail: exit the stopped leg, then follow the profit of what is left (2026-11-19)
**Context.** On 19 Nov 2026 a paper short strangle (BTC-SSTG-19NOV26-0736) had a leg stop at twice the entry on each leg and a trailing target (ADR-103) of +17.96 USD with a 1.20 USD give-back. The call reached its stop and exited alone at a loss of 26.51 USD; the put, sold at 255.9, was in profit. The trader expected the trail to take the put over. It could not: the target is judged on the strategy P&L, realised loss included, and the put's whole premium (25.59 USD) was less than the call's loss, so +17.96 USD was out of reach from the moment the stop fired. The put sat open with nothing following its profit until the trader stopped the paper trade by hand 25 minutes later (audit: `strategy.rule_fire` 04:04 UTC by the engine, `strategy.stop_archive` 04:29 UTC by the user). The trader asked for the surviving leg to be trailed, as an option, for any strategy, with nothing changing for rules that do not choose it.
**Decision.** (1) A leg stop's "What to exit" gains a third choice beside "this leg only" and "the whole strategy": "this leg, then trail the rest". It is stored on the leg stop itself as a give-back (`trail` on a leg stop with scope `leg`; the `trail_trigger` / `trail_value` columns of migration 0027, so no new migration); the scope values do not change. (2) A fired rule never re-arms, so the trail is its own rule: when such a stop fires, its leg is confirmed out and legs remain open, the engine arms one rule of a new kind `trail`. Its level (`threshold_usd`) is the strategy's realised total at that moment. Where the rest stands is the strategy P&L less that level: the unrealised P&L of the legs still open, counted from their own entries (so a put already 10.59 USD in profit starts at +10.59, not at zero), plus anything they realise later; the stopped leg's loss is not in it, and a later trim moves its result into the realised total without moving the figure. The trail is armed last in the fire, after the realised total it starts from is stored, and only when this fire itself booked the leg. (3) The trail keeps the best standing (`peak_pnl`, whole cents rounded down, written only upwards, the same step as ADR-103) and exits every open leg when the rest falls to the best less the give-back. The give-back is a percentage of the best or money (USD internally). A money give-back starts at once, even under water; a percentage has nothing to take a share of until the rest is at least a cent in profit, so it waits until then, and it never amounts to less than 0.10 USD (`REST_TRAIL_MIN_GIVE_USD`): 15 % of a best of a few cents is a fraction of a cent, which the next flicker would cross. (4) The dialog offers 10 % by default. The trader first proposed 5 %; on their own trade (put 10.59 USD in profit when the call stopped, 100 lots = 0.1 BTC) 5 % is 0.53 USD, five points on the option, which ordinary flicker crosses at once and so behaves like "exit the whole strategy"; 15 % (16 points) was agreed first on 19 Nov 2026 and, after the trader ran it on a paper strangle the same day (best 9.39 USD, exit at 7.98 USD, 1.41 USD given back), lowered to 10 % (about 11 points on the example above) at their request, editable per rule. (5) One trail per strategy: a second leg stop set to trail that fires while one is running exits its leg and says the trail is already running. Its realised result then counts as a fall of the rest. (6) No trail starts while the stopped leg's exit is unconfirmed (resting, or possibly not delivered): the leg may still be held, so "the rest" is not yet defined; the fired note says so. (7) The trail is judged after the protective kinds and before the target (`RULE_KIND_ORDER`), so a remaining leg's own stop still wins a tick that crosses both. Its exit books the reason `stopped` (a trailing stop is a stop; the total can be a loss) and the chip reads "trail on the rest fired". (8) Only the engine arms a trail. The rule-set route accepts kind `trail` only while one is running: it keeps the level, takes a new give-back, and removes the trail when the set leaves it out; a trail with none running is refused (400). The best is kept only while the give-back is unchanged: a tighter give-back, or a percentage put under a negative best, would set the exit level above where the rest stands and fire on the next tick, so a changed give-back starts the trail again from where the rest stands (the next tick sets the best). The Protect dialog shows the running trail as its own box, says so when the give-back is changed, and ticks the box by itself when a trail starts while the dialog is open. These four points (the restart, the minimum, arming last, arming only on a booked exit) came out of the code review of 19 Nov 2026.
**Consequences.** Rules that do not choose it are untouched: a leg stop without a give-back fires as before, and rows written before this decision carry none.
