The target trail of ADR-103 keeps its behaviour; the engine's best-keeping step is now shared by both. `RULE_KINDS` grows by one, so every table keyed by kind names it (labels, order, close reason, fired chip); the schema test that pins the judging order changed with it. The exit is at the mark of the tick that saw the fall, as for every rule, so a gap can book less than the exit level. The backtest's exit rules do not model the choice (they are a separate implementation in `packages/pricing`). Traceability HC-TR-212 (schema, route, engine) and HC-TR-213 (dialog, card, chip). Left out: GAPS #141.

## ADR-105 · The pane follows a closed trade too, and a trade says when it opened and closed (2026-11-19)
**Context.** ADR-026 let the analysis pane follow a paper or live strategy. A closed (archived) trade was left out: the card on the Closed chip looked and behaved like a button (hover, title "Click to analyse this strategy in the pane"), the click set the pane source, and the pane's own bar then threw it out as "gone" because it only looked for status paper or live. The pane fell back to the Builder draft, so the trader who clicked a closed short strangle saw a long strangle they had never traded. On 19 Nov 2026 the trader reported exactly this with 98 closed trades and none open: every click failed. The card also gave the start and the close as a UTC date with no time, so a trade opened and closed the same day read "19 Nov 2026 · 19 Nov 2026", and a trade opened at 02:00 IST read as the day before.
**Decision.** (1) The pane follows any strategy that is not a draft. For a closed trade it analyses every leg that was actually entered, at its entry premium, as the Details payoff already does; it subscribes to no quotes (the contracts may have settled and left the venue) and a workbench draft is never applied to it. (2) The hook says so (`closed`: start, close, reason, final result), and the panels act on it: the Following bar shows the Archived tag, the close time, the reason and the final result; Payoff keeps the tiles and the expiry curve, replaces the live strip with one sentence (opened, closed, why, final result, what the curve is), and drops the target curve and controls, the IV and expected-move layers, open interest, probability of profit and the Greeks strip; Greeks, Scenarios and Ladder say "This trade is closed" instead of pricing a dead contract at a default volatility. Vol and Structure read the market, not the trade, and stay. A closed trade is drawn intrinsic for every leg (no later leg keeps time value), runs no scenario passes, shows no probability in Share, and a closed trade in which no leg was ever entered says so. The bar's memory of having seen the strategy open ends when the pane stops following a strategy, so a trade that closed after the trader left it is shown on the first click of its closed card (found in the code review of 19 Nov 2026, with the month name: the formatter takes the month from our own table because engines print "Sep" or "Sept"). (3) What ADR-026 did on a close is kept: a strategy that closes while the pane follows it open hands the pane back to the Builder (the bar remembers that it saw the strategy open); only a closed trade the trader clicks on purpose stays. A closed trade is never auto-followed. (4) Times: one formatter (`fmtDateTime`) gives the day and the minute in the viewer's own time zone with the zone named ("19 Nov 2026, 12:42 IST"); the card's started and closed lines and the Details statistics use it, and Details gains a Closed figure for an archived trade.
**Consequences.** Display only: no route, schema or order path changes, and Adjust, Protect and Go live stay unavailable for a closed trade. The card's date can differ by a day from what it showed before for trades near midnight UTC, because it is now the viewer's day. Traceability HC-TR-214 (pane) and HC-TR-215 (times). Left out: GAPS #142.

## ADR-106 · The workspace is the Desk at /desk; the account pages are /plans and /invite (2026-11-21)
**Context.** The product was built for parity with the competitor's site, and the address of its main screen came with it: the workspace lived at `/analyse` and was called "Analyse" in the header, the shortcuts sheet and the copy, exactly as on the original; the account pages were `/subscription` and `/referrals` under the menu labels "My Subscription" and "My Referrals", also the original's. A review of the competitor's current site on 21 Nov 2026 confirmed they still use those paths and labels. The owner asked that nothing a visitor sees should read as copied, and that the old word should go wherever it appears, pictures included.
**Decision.** (1) The workspace is the **Desk** at `/desk`; the account pages are `/plans` and `/invite`, with the menu labels "My plan" and "Invite friends" and the header tabs "Plans" and "Invite". `/auth` keeps its name (the owner's choice). (2) No redirect is kept from the earlier addresses: the product had no users when it was renamed (it was not yet deployed), so nothing outside the team links to them, and an earlier address is simply not found. (3) The rename goes all the way through what we own and ship: the route folders, the header variant, the store fields (`deskCollapse`, `followPositions`), test ids, the e2e spec (`desk.spec.ts`), the screenshot and baseline file names (`desk-*.png`), the links the API sends (alerts, rule exits, playbook proposals), the installed app's start address, the feature records, the generated spec and guide, and the design docs. Copy that used the word as a verb now says what happens ("see this strategy in the pane"). (4) API paths under `/v1` are not page addresses and keep their names.
**Consequences.** A persisted pane-collapse preference under the old store field is dropped once (the pane opens uncollapsed). The header text changed, so the Linux pixel baselines of every workspace picture need a refresh from the CI baseline job; the baseline files were renamed with their old pixels. Traceability HC-SH-142. Not done here, recorded in GAPS #143: the design mock in `mockup-v2/` keeps the old word in its own code and file names, earlier entries of this file and of GAPS.md keep the names that were true when they were written, and `mockup-clone/` is untouched until the IP cleanup removes it.

## ADR-107 · The workspace needs a plan when no Free plan is on offer; a manual grant carries a payment note (2026-11-21)
**Context.** The owner does not want a free tier at launch: nobody should use the product without paying, the choice must be reversible without a release, and the admin must be able to add someone who paid outside the app (bank transfer, UPI, cash). Most of this already existed. The "Free plan" is not a special object: it is whichever active plan costs ₹0 a month (ADR-030), so switching that plan off in Admin → Plans removes the free tier and switching it on restores it. An admin could already put a user on any plan without payment, one at a time or in bulk, change the validity and invite a user (ADR-032), all audited. What was missing: with Free off, a user without a plan could no longer trade, save or set alerts, but the workspace itself still opened; the banner still said "Your free plan is active"; and a manual grant had no place to say how the user paid.
**Decision.** (1) `GET /v1/plan` and the plan inside `GET /v1/subscription` answer one more question, `access`: true when the user holds a running subscription, or a Free plan is on offer, or the user is an admin (an admin is never locked out of the product they run). (2) The Desk's server layout asks it and sends a signed-in visitor without access to `/plans?need=plan`. Only a clear "no" closes the Desk: when the plan cannot be read the page opens, and the trading routes still enforce the plan as before. (3) The plans page says a plan is needed and that a direct payment appears as soon as it is added; the banner says "You have no active plan" in place of the free-plan line. (4) A manual grant (single or bulk) takes an optional payment note of up to 200 characters, stored with the audit entry and shown in the user's History tab. (5) No new setting and no migration: the switch is the ₹0 plan's Active toggle. The seed keeps its Free plan active, because the test suites build on it; production switches it off in the admin.
**Consequences.** A subscriber whose plan lapses is locked out of the Desk while Free is off (that is the point), and gets back in on renewal or a grant. Market Analytics and the Terminal stay open, as they already are to visitors who are not signed in. Traceability HC-AC-075 (access) and HC-AD-130 (payment note). Left out: GAPS #144.

## ADR-108 · Autopilot: the server enters paper trades by itself (2026-11-21)
**Context.** ADR-098 and ADR-102 stopped at proposals: the system detects that a playbook's plan would enter and the trader opens it by hand, "never auto-place". The owner now wants the strategies proven faster and without a hand on every entry: on paper, take every opportunity, never the same trade again within 15 minutes, count the result after the entry and exit fees, and try two ways of taking a winner off. A review of the competitor on 21 Nov 2026 showed clock-timed live "algos" with no market check; ours should enter on a condition, not on a time of day. The owner also asked to be told when a better entry appears while a trade is open, and to see how entries close to expiry behave, where premiums are small against the fees and the capital.
**Decision.** (1) **Paper only.** Autopilot writes paper strategies; it has no path to a venue's order surface, and "never auto-place" stays true for live. A trade it opened cannot be taken live, alone or in a batch (`/live/place` and `/live/batch` refuse it): its exits were priced for the paper entry and must never send a real order. Going live with it needs its own ADR, a verified margin figure (GAPS #109) and a compliance answer. (2) **When it looks.** After every market snapshot (five minutes by default) the engine takes the recorded history with that snapshot as "today" and asks each playbook, through the same `entryFor` the backtest and the proposals use, whether its plan enters. The entry is priced at that snapshot's marks, so the signal and the fill come from the same prices. A snapshot older than twelve minutes is not entered on. (3) **Who gets a trade.** Every active trader with a plan (ADR-107: a running subscription, a Free plan on offer, or an admin) who switched Autopilot on and the playbook on. One open Autopilot trade per trader, playbook, venue and asset, held by a partial unique index against two passes; after a trade ends the same key waits 15 minutes. A trade whose legs the trader closed by hand is closed out by the next pass (dated by its last leg, its rules disarmed), so it starts the cool-down and does not block the playbook. The structure and its levels are sized with the trader's own lot size on the default venue, as the rules engine values the trade. The control never trades. A structure with a futures leg (the covered call) is left out until the margin figure is verified. (4) **Exits, armed at entry as ordinary rules** so the existing engine closes the trade. The target level is 10 % of the most the structure can make, plus the entry fee and an exit fee of the same size under the venue's fee rule, so reaching it is that profit after both fees. The trader picks per playbook: `target` closes there; `trail` keeps the trade from there and closes when it gives back 10 % of its best (ADR-103). The playbook's own stop (a share of the maximum loss) and time exit are armed with it. No plan, no trade: when the target is not below the maximum profit the fees eat the trade, which is logged as the reason. (5) **The log.** Every entry with its figures (spot, days to expiry, premium, maximum profit and loss, fees, capital, target, stop); every reason a switched-on playbook did not enter, written once per change of reason; and a note when, with a trade open, the very same structure (expiry, strikes and sides) prices at least 10 % better than that trade's entry (at most one note per 15 minutes). Lines are kept 90 days. (6) **No budget and no caps in this first version**, by the owner's choice for the proving phase; the capital each entry ties up is logged so the budget rules can be set from evidence. Autopilot's trades do not use the trader's monthly paper allowance. (7) Settings live in their own table (`autopilot_settings`), the log in `autopilot_runs`, and a strategy Autopilot opened carries `autopilot_playbook_id` (migration 0028).
**Consequences.** A trader sees Autopilot's trades in the Paper list, named "<playbook> · Autopilot" and tagged, and can close or edit them like any other. Paper results will not match the playbook's backtest record: the record judges exits once a day on recorded marks with the playbook's own target, Autopilot judges every two seconds with a 10 % target. With a playbook's condition true all day, a trade that closes re-enters after the cool-down, which is the many-opportunities behaviour asked for and also means a stopped trade can be followed by another. Traceability HC-TR-216 (settings), HC-TR-217 (entries), HC-TR-218 (log and notes). Left out: GAPS #145.

## ADR-109 · Autopilot has its own screen, a tag on its trades and a place on the Desk's status bar (2026-11-21)
**Context.** ADR-108 gave Autopilot an engine and an API and no screen: the owner switched it on in the database and saw its trades only as more cards in the Paper list. The owner asked for a screen in the product, for a summary of Autopilot on the Desk, for Autopilot's trades to be told apart from manual ones, and whether strategies that share a strike should go to separate sub-accounts.
**Decision.** (1) **A page of its own at `/autopilot`**, in the header next to the Desk and in the command palette, behind the same sign-in and plan check as the Desk. It is about every strategy at once, not about one trade, so it is not a tab of the workspace. (2) **What it shows.** The master switch (off means no new entries; the choices are kept). A summary: open trades, the capital they tie up (the most they can lose, from the log), today's result (open trades and those closed today, before fees), strategies switched on. One card per tradable playbook with its switch, how a winner is taken off (trail or target), its record per coin, and what it is doing now: in a trade with its P&L, or its newest reason for not entering. The trades Autopilot holds, each with a health reading, its exit levels read from the rules armed at entry, Exit now (the Paper list's own stop dialog) and Open on the Desk. The log. (3) **Health** is three steps: in profit, on track; in loss, losing; in loss with three quarters of the stop distance used, close to its stop. A trade with no stop (its risk is the premium paid) is never "close to its stop". Health and P&L are shown only while every open leg has a live price: a missing quote reads as the entry price elsewhere in the Paper list, which here would show a losing trade as on track, so the screen says "No live price" instead. (4) **Stop everything** switches Autopilot off, then reads again what it holds (a pass may have entered since the page last read) and squares each trade off at the current prices, one after the other, after a confirmation. It waits for the feed, never closes a trade at its entry price for want of a quote (that trade is left open and named in the dialog), carries on past a trade the server refuses to close and names that one too, and never touches the trader's own trades. (5) **Telling them apart.** A strategy says which playbook Autopilot opened it for (`autopilotPlaybookId`); the Paper card carries an Autopilot tag and its Go live is disabled with the reason. An Autopilot trade is closed, never parked as a draft: the stop dialog offers no draft for it and the API refuses one (a draft has no close time, so the 15-minute wait would not start, and it could be started again by hand carrying Autopilot's mark). (6) **On the Desk** the status bar always shows Autopilot's state and its open trades, with their P&L once every leg has a live price, and links to the screen (the Desk's own header has no tabs, so this is the way in besides the palette). It re-reads each minute only while Autopilot is on. (7) **Strikes shared between strategies.** On paper every strategy is its own book, so two strategies on one strike do not affect each other. On a venue they net inside one account, so the day Autopilot is allowed to trade live it must trade through an account (sub-account, ADR-068) of its own and never through the one the trader uses by hand; that is a condition of the live ADR, recorded in GAPS #146.
**Consequences.** The screen prices Autopilot's trades in the browser like the Paper list does, so its P&L needs the page open and the feed live; the engine's own exits do not. The server opens and closes these trades by itself, so the screen re-reads the strategies list every minute with the log (the Desk's bar while Autopilot is on); a list that could not be read is an error, never "no trades". A card states that the record shown is of the playbook's own exits while Autopilot takes profit its own way. No budget, caps or sizing yet (ADR-108 point 6). Traceability HC-TR-219 (screen and strategies), HC-TR-220 (running trades, health, exits), HC-SH-143 (the Desk bar, the card tag, navigation).

## ADR-110 · Autopilot holds several trades of one strategy when the strikes differ (2026-11-21)
**Context.** ADR-108 held one open Autopilot trade per trader, playbook, venue and asset, and wrote a note when the same structure priced better. In the proving phase the owner wants more to judge on: when a playbook's entry moves to other strikes (the spot moved, another expiry became the right one) while a trade is open, take that one too, and watch how each works.
**Decision.** (1) **One trade per structure.** A trade's structure is every leg's side, kind, strike and expiry. The very structure a trade holds is never entered twice; a clearly better price for it stays a note on that trade (ADR-108 point 5), now limited per trade and not per playbook. (2) **Other strikes or another expiry is a new trade** of the same playbook, through every check an entry goes through (plan, fresh prices, a price on every leg, a target above the fees). (3) **Bounds.** At most `AUTOPILOT_MAX_OPEN` (5) trades per trader, playbook, venue and asset; a sixth is a logged reason. The 15-minute wait now runs from the key's newest entry or close, whichever trade it was: one entry per wait, and none right after a close, so a strategy cannot re-take a trade it has just left by entering on the neighbouring strikes at once. (4) **Storage.** `strategies.autopilot_structure` holds the structure and the partial unique index gains it (migration 0029), so two passes still cannot open the same trade twice. The engine compares against that stored structure, the one the trade was entered on, so a leg the trader rolls or adds by hand does not turn it into another trade; a row from before the column has none (the index does not cover it) and gets it from its legs on the next pass. (5) **The screen** adds a strategy's trades on one coin up on its card (how many, their P&L together, the worst health) and shows each trade's strikes and expiry on its row.
**Consequences.** Paper capital in use can reach five times a structure's risk per strategy and coin: there is still no budget (ADR-108 point 6), which is acceptable on paper only and is one more reason the live step needs its own decision. A playbook whose strikes follow the spot (the butterfly, the spreads) will fill its five places on a moving day; the cap and the wait are the only brakes. A trade with a leg closed by a leg rule keeps the structure it was entered on. Traceability HC-TR-221 (engine), HC-TR-222 (screen); GAPS #147.

## ADR-111 · Autopilot results: what its trades came to, by strategy, exit style and days to expiry (2026-11-21)
**Context.** The proving phase (ADR-108, ADR-110) exists to answer three questions: which strategies make money on paper, whether a winner is better trailed or closed at its target, and whether entries close to expiry (small premiums, fees large against them) pay. The screen showed the open trades and a log; the answers needed a query by hand.
**Decision.** (1) **`GET /v1/autopilot/results`** reads every entry the log still holds (90 days) joined to its strategy. An open trade is counted. A closed trade adds its P&L at the marks it was closed on (the strategy's realised P&L), less the fees estimated at entry (the entry fill plus an exit fill of the same size under the venue's rule, as logged with the entry): the paper book charges no fee, so the net is what a venue would have left. A win is a closed trade whose net is above zero. (2) **Groups.** By playbook, asset and exit style (open, closed, won, gross, fees, net, best, worst, mean time held, how they ended by close reason); by the days to expiry at entry in four bands (expiry day or the day before, 2 to 7, 8 to 30, more than 30); a total; and the newest 100 closed trades with their strikes. (3) **Money** is summed in whole cents and returned with both decimals. (3a) **Nothing drops out by hand.** A trade Autopilot opened cannot be deleted (409), so a trader cannot improve the results by removing the losers; a trade whose legs were all squared off by hand counts as closed, dated by its last leg, even while its row still says paper. (4) **The screen** shows the total, trail against target over every strategy, the two tables and the newest 25 closed trades under the strategies, re-read each minute.
**Consequences.** The fee is an estimate made at entry, not a fill: a trade closed at settlement would in fact pay a different (settlement) fee, and one closed leg by leg pays per leg. The exit style is the one logged at entry; changing a card's style affects later trades only. Results older than the log's retention drop out. No chart and no export yet. Traceability HC-TR-223 (API), HC-TR-224 (screen); GAPS #148.

## ADR-112 · Autopilot trades four evening strangles by the clock, and holds one trade at a time again (2026-11-21)
**Context.** The owner asked for a backtest of everything Autopilot runs and of the structures a competitor projects, wanted a minimum reward to risk of 1 to 3, the nearest daily expiry, an entry around 21:00 IST after the US open, and asked me to confirm that taking the same strategy again on other strikes (ADR-110) pays. The study (`docs/research/autopilot-daily-study.md`: 106 BTC daily expiries, 8 August to 21 November 2026, 5-minute marks, the exchange's real fees, 19 structures at 24 entry hours) answered: (a) only sold strangles earned after fees, and only at the 20:30 and 21:30 IST bars; (b) the structures that offer 3 to 1 or better (bought butterflies) lost in every month, so a reward-to-risk floor would keep the losers and drop the winners; (c) the ladder lost on every structure: the trades it added lost on average, because the price reached the new strike by trending.
**Decision.** (1) **Four playbooks entered by the clock** (`CLOCK_PLAYBOOKS` in `packages/pricing`), BTC only, 100 lots, once a day in the hour from 16:00 UTC (21:30 IST; Autopilot enters on its first pass inside the window, which is the 16:00 UTC bar every figure of the study quoted on the cards is for), on Delta India only, on the first expiry at least half a day and at most a day and a half out (the next settlement, never a later one when that one has no prices): a strangle sold three strikes out with wings bought eight out, held to settlement; the same five and ten out; a naked strangle three out with each sold option bought back at twice its price; the same at three times. (2) **Their own exits only.** No Autopilot profit level, no strategy stop: a winged strangle has no rule at all (the settler closes it), a naked one has a `leg_stop` rule per sold option. The exit style `own` records this; a trader cannot choose it for another playbook, and whatever the card sends for a clock playbook is saved as `own`. (3) **Entry checks**: inside the window, fresh prices, the next expiry priced, a price on every leg, a premium larger than both fees, not already entered within six hours, and no trade of the key open. (4) **One trade at a time again**: `AUTOPILOT_MAX_OPEN` is 1. The ladder's code and index stay (a test still exercises five); trades it opened run to their exits. (5) **No reward-to-risk filter**: the loss is bounded by the wings or by the stops instead. (6) **Outside the recorded list**: clock playbooks are not in `PLAYBOOKS`, so the six-hourly record (which enters at the settlement hour, the worst hour of the study) and the Desk's proposals do not see them; Autopilot's own results (ADR-111) are their record. (7) **Paper only**, as all of Autopilot: a naked strangle on a venue needs margin this build does not compute, and the capital figure of a naked trade is empty for that reason.
**Consequences.** The four were chosen after seeing 456 combinations on the same 106 days, so their backtest figures are in-sample and optimistic; the card says "Backtest only" and quotes the figure with its sample. Three and a half months of one asset is a short record: August lost with the stop, and the wide-wing version has prices on about half the days only. Strikes are "three listed strikes out", which was 600 on the daily ladder of the study and is whatever the ladder lists on the day (ADR-006). Marks, not bids and offers, in the study and in the paper fills. Traceability HC-TR-225 (playbooks and engine), HC-TR-226 (screen); GAPS #149.

## ADR-113 · A fifth evening strangle that takes its profit at half the premium (2026-11-21)
**Context.** The four evening strangles of ADR-112 have no profit exit: they are held to settlement behind wings or stops. The owner, seeing them in profit two hours in, asked for a quick-profit version to run beside them.
**Decision.** `evening-strangle-take-half`: the stop-at-double strangle (sold three strikes out, each sold option bought back at twice its price) plus one rule, a target on the whole trade at half the premium collected (`clock.takeProfitOfCredit` 0.5, armed as an ordinary `target` rule with no trail). Same entry, window, size and stops as `evening-strangle-stop-2x`, so the two differ only in taking the profit early and the results compare them directly. Its exit style is `own` like the others.
**Consequences.** Run on the study's data after the request (`study.py`, "stop 2x + take half"): +6.75 a day on 106 days against +7.51 for the stop alone, 59 % of days positive against 45 %, the same worst day (-60), and the only one of the five naked versions of that strangle (hold, stop at 2x, stop at 3x, each stop with the take) positive in each of the four months (August +175, September +242, October +5, November +293 against -53, +488, +26, +334 for the stop alone). The same take on the 3x stop averaged more (+9.27 a day, 77 % of days positive, worst day -82) and lost October (-37); the 2x was chosen because it differs from an existing playbook by one rule only, which is the comparison the owner asked for. So it gives up a little of the average for steadier months: in-sample like the rest. The target is judged on the whole trade's P&L at marks, a leg already stopped included, as the study did; it is not entered when half the premium rounds to nothing. Differences from the study: the engine judges it every two seconds on live marks (the study on 5-minute closes), so a brief dip takes the profit where the study held, and paper realised P&L carries no fee where the study charged the stopped leg's exit. An early close pays the exit fills a settled option does not. Traceability HC-TR-227; GAPS #149.

## ADR-114 · A sixth evening strangle that trails the other side after a stop (2026-11-22)
**Context.** On a sold strangle with a stop on each option, one stop leaves the other option open to settlement. The owner asked whether that side should be trailed or closed, since a reversal can stop it too. Tested on the study's 106 days (`study.py`, section E; a stop was hit on 64 of them with the stop at double): keeping the other side to settlement averaged +7.51 a day with a worst day of -60; trailing it with a give-back of 50 % of its best +6.45 and -42; give-backs of 30 % and 10 % +5.50 and +5.81; closing it at once +6.32 and -42. With the stop at triple the choice made no difference (+11.65 to +12.19, worst day about -98).
**Decision.** `evening-strangle-trail-rest`: the stop-at-double strangle whose two leg stops are set to "this leg, then trail the rest" (ADR-104) with a give-back of 50 % (`clock.trailRestPct`). It differs from `evening-strangle-stop-2x` in this rule only, so the results compare keeping against trailing. The existing playbooks keep the other side, which earned the most.
**Consequences.** The trail runs on the engine's own rule: it starts when the rest is a cent in profit, its give-back is never under the floor of ADR-104, and it is judged every two seconds on live marks where the study used 5-minute closes, so it will close earlier than the study did on a flicker. In-sample like the rest. Traceability HC-TR-228; GAPS #149.

## ADR-115 · Autopilot enters weekly strangles on Fridays and Tuesdays (2026-11-22)
**Context.** The owner asked about weekly and monthly expiries. A second study (`study_weekly.py`, `results-weekly-2026-11-22.txt`: 51 BTC Friday expiries, 5 December 2025 to 20 November 2026, hourly marks, real fees) found: a strangle sold 3 % or 5 % away a week before expiry (Friday for the next Friday) earned under every exit with a stop, in both halves of the year (3 % away, stop at triple: +40.65 a week, worst week -168), and lost 1,220 in its worst week with no stop; the same trade entered two to four days before was about flat; the 11 monthly expiries give one to seven trades per entry day, which is no evidence. The owner then asked to run every weekly variant, like the daily ones, on both Friday and Tuesday entries.
**Decision.** (1) **Ten weekly playbooks** (`WEEKLY_PLAYBOOKS`), BTC on Delta India, 100 lots, sold 3 % away: five exits (wings bought 7 % away and held; stop at double; stop at triple; stop at double with the take at half the premium; stop at double then trail the rest by half) each entered on a Friday for next Friday's expiry and on a Tuesday for that week's Friday expiry, in the hour from 16:00 UTC. The Tuesday set is run although the study has it about flat, as a check of the backtest on live prices. No monthly playbook. (2) **The clock gains a day of the week** (`clock.weekdayUtc`), and the entry's expiry is bounded on both sides (`minDte`, `clock.maxDte`: 6.5 to 7.5 days from a Friday, 2.5 to 3.5 from a Tuesday), so a daily expiry or the week after is never taken instead. (3) **A strike a percentage away.** A template leg may name `pct` instead of a count of strikes: the listed strike nearest the spot moved by that percentage (still a strike the venue lists, ADR-006), refused when the ladder does not reach that far. A weekly ladder is 500 apart where a daily one is 200, so "three strikes out" would not be the same trade. (4) **The screen groups the cards** in three families: longer-dated, evening (daily expiry), weekly.
**Consequences.** One trade a week per playbook: ten weeks give ten results each, so the weekly record builds slowly beside the daily one. Up to ten more naked or winged strangles are open at once on paper; no budget bounds them (ADR-108 point 6). The percentage strike uses the snapshot's spot, where the count of strikes used the at-the-money row. In-sample like the rest; the winged version had prices on about half the weeks. Traceability HC-TR-229 (playbooks, strike rule, engine), HC-TR-230 (screen); GAPS #150.

## ADR-116 · The Autopilot screen is a table narrowed by expiry and by coin (2026-11-22)
**Context.** With six longer-dated, six daily and ten weekly strategies the screen was twenty-two cards of three paragraphs each, about four screens tall, with the figures a trader compares (average, share of wins, worst result) inside sentences, no way to look at one expiry family or one coin, and the results at the far end. On a phone the page scrolled sideways. The owner asked for the screen to be reworked with a separation between daily, weekly and monthly and between BTC, ETH and XAUT, ahead of monthly strategies and more coins.
**Decision.** (1) **Two selections** in a bar under the summary: expiry (All, Trend, Daily, Weekly, and Monthly once a monthly strategy exists) and coin (All and each coin a strategy trades). They narrow the strategies, the running trades, the log and the results together; the summary stays everything and the bar says so. (2) **One row per strategy** in place of a card, in a table per family: the switch and name with when it enters, how it gets out in a few words with the exit style, its backtest as figures (mean per trade, share won, worst, sample), its paper result so far (closed, won, net after estimated fees, from the results), and per coin what it is doing now. The description, the full exit text and the backtest sentence open under "What it does". (3) **Where the figures come from.** `playbookCadence` and `playbookBacktest` in `packages/pricing`: the cadence follows from the clock, and the backtest figures of a clock playbook are data beside its evidence sentence, with a test that the two agree. A recorded playbook has no such figure: its live record per coin stays under Now. (4) **The results panel** takes the selection: its rows, totals and closed trades follow it, and the days-to-expiry table, which the server adds up over everything, is left out for a selection. (5) **The header's tabs scroll inside the header on a phone**, so no page of the default header is wider than the screen.
**Consequences.** The selection is not kept across visits and is not in the address. Every strategy is still listed for "All", so the page is long until a family is chosen. I proposed three tabs (strategies, running, results) and kept one page instead: the running trades beside the strategies are the point of the screen, and the selection already shortens it. Traceability HC-TR-231 (cadence and figures), HC-TR-232 (screen); GAPS #151.

## ADR-117 · Strikes by premium, and three monthly strangles that take half (2026-11-22)
**Context.** The owner described how a competitor's automation picks strikes: by the premium each side pays (about 100 for the daily expiry, 200 for the weekly, 1,000 for the monthly), the monthly one entered between the 20th and the 10th for the month-end expiry two months out; and told me to test and keep whatever works, not to copy. Three runs (`study_premium.py` on the daily and weekly caches, `study_monthly.py` on the eleven monthly expiries of 2026 over the 92 days each is listed): (a) **daily by premium**: positive at every level from 50 to 400 a side with a stop, in both halves of the period; the mean and the worst day both grow with the premium (100 a side, stop at 3x and take half: +4.25 a day, 84 % of days positive, worst -55; 300 a side, stop at 3x: +10.79, worst -148); (b) **weekly by premium**: Friday entries positive with a stop at every level (200 a side, 3x and take half: +7.26 a week, 78 %, worst -68; 500 a side, 3x: +20.74, worst -131), Tuesday entries flat or negative at every level, and with no stop every level lost over 1,000 in its worst week; (c) **monthly**: held to settlement with no stop, or with wings, every shape LOST (1,000 a side, 60 to 75 days out: -139 a trade); with a stop on each option and the whole trade closed at half the premium every shape was positive in 7 to 10 of the 11 expiries.
**Decision.** (1) **A template leg may name `premium`**: the listed out-of-the-money strike whose mark is nearest it, refused when nothing is priced within half of it (ADR-006 holds: a listed strike). (2) **Two premium strangles** beside the existing sets, each with a stop at 3x and the take at half: 100 a side every evening, 200 a side each Friday. No Tuesday version. (3) **Three monthly strangles**, each with a stop on every sold option and the take at half the premium, never held with no stop and never with wings: 1,000 a side on the month-end expiry 60 to 75 days out (the competitor's shape), 8 % away at the same range (the best average, the largest single losses), and 12 % away on the expiry 30 to 44 days out (the steadiest). (4) **Once per expiry.** A monthly window is open every evening while an expiry is in range; the engine enters on the first one and never takes that expiry again for the key, even after the take has closed it. (5) BTC on Delta India, 100 lots, paper only, the `own` exit style, like the other clock playbooks.
**Consequences.** Eleven expiries is a thin record: the monthly figures are means over every entry day of a range, which overlap, so the count of positive expiries is the figure to read. A monthly trade ties its key up for weeks unless the take closes it. All in-sample. The premium selector reads the snapshot's marks, so a stale far-strike mark moves the strike. Traceability HC-TR-233; GAPS #152.

## ADR-118 · The monthly set gets every exit, and wings with a stop (2026-11-22)
**Context.** ADR-117 put three monthly strangles on paper, all with a stop and the take at half, and tested wings only held to settlement (where they lost). The owner asked why the monthly expiry did not get every option the daily and weekly sets have, wings included. Rerun with every exit on the winged shapes too (`study_monthly.py`, the same eleven expiries): wings held to settlement lose as before, but **wings with a stop on the sold options** was the best monthly result by far, because the wing behind a stopped option is kept and pays when the month keeps trending. Sold 12 % away with wings 18 % away, entered 30 to 44 days before, each sold option stopped at three times its price: +209.1 a trade, 92 % of entries positive, positive in all 11 expiries; sold 8 % with wings 12 %, the same stop and the take at half: +54.8, 98 %, 11 of 11.
**Decision.** (1) **Seven more monthly playbooks** on the shape entered one month out: for 12 % away the stop at double, the stop at triple, the stop at double then a trail on the rest, wings held (a check: its backtest is negative), wings with the stop at triple, wings with the stop at triple and the take at half; and 8 % away with wings, the stop at triple and the take at half. With the three of ADR-117 that is ten. (2) **A monthly playbook trades month-end expiries only**: `isMonthEndExpiry` (the last Friday of the month) is applied when the expiry is chosen, so another expiry listed in the range of days neither is taken nor hides the month-end behind it. (3) **The figures read per trade.** A monthly backtest is the mean over every entry day of a range, and the days of one expiry overlap, so the card says "a trade", gives the entry days, the expiries and how many of those were positive, and the evidence says Autopilot takes the first evening only.
**Consequences.** The winged result rests on eleven expiries of a year with large one-way months, on wing prices that are model marks with little trading behind them, and on a stop judged on hourly closes: it is the figure most likely to shrink on paper. Ten monthly rows are open at once at most, each tying its key up until its trade ends. Traceability HC-TR-233; GAPS #152.

## ADR-119 · Autopilot on ETH and XAUT: each coin from its own backtest, on its own clock (2026-11-22)
**Context.** Every clock playbook was BTC only. The owner asked for the daily, weekly and monthly sets on ETH and XAUT, "the same or whichever tests better", then pointed out that Gold expires at 9:30 PM IST and has no weekend expiry. The first XAUT study had reused BTC's settlement hour (12:00 UTC); XAUT options settle at 16:00 UTC and list no Sunday expiry, so every figure of it was wrong and was thrown away. Reruns, each coin on its own settlement time, lot and strike spacing (`study_premium.py`, `study_monthly.py`; tables `results-daily-ETH`, `results-daily-XAUT`, `results-entry-hours-*`, `results-weekly-ETH`, `results-weekly-XAUT`, `results-monthly-ETH` of 22 Nov 2026):
- **ETH daily** behaves like BTC: only the evening earns (best at 21:30 IST), sold 1 % away with each option stopped at three times its price +4.46 a day (61 % of 106 days positive, worst -50); with no stop +6.67 and a worst day of -138.
- **XAUT daily**: of 23 entry hours the hour after the previous expiry (21:30 IST, a full day before the next) is the best, and thin: sold 1 % away, stop at double with the take at half, +0.19 a day on 91 days (67 %, worst -1.4); an hour later +0.12, two hours later +0.09, and from then to the next evening flat or negative. With wings about half as much (+0.06 to +0.11 on 77 days).
- **ETH weekly**: Friday entries sold 4 % away, stop at triple +14.43 a week (57 % of 51 weeks, worst -83), with the take at half +14.08 (75 %, worst -72), both positive in both halves of the year; unlike BTC the Tuesday entry earned (3 % away, stop at triple, +9.03).
- **XAUT weekly**: a price in 16 or 17 of 51 weeks, none a full week ahead, flat to negative. **XAUT monthly**: no option prices before the middle of 2026. Neither has a playbook.
- **ETH monthly**: as on BTC, held to settlement loses and a stop pays; wings with a stop on the sold options is the strongest shape (16 % away with wings 24 %, 60 to 75 days out: +88.30 a trade, 10 of 11 expiries positive).
**Decision.** (1) **Twenty playbooks of their own** (`ALT_DAILY_PLAYBOOKS`, `ALT_WEEKLY_PLAYBOOKS`, `ALT_MONTHLY_PLAYBOOKS` in `packages/pricing/src/playbooks.ts`): six ETH and four XAUT evening strangles, five ETH weekly (four Friday, one Tuesday), five ETH monthly. Each trades one coin and carries that coin's figures. (2) **Strikes by percentage of the spot**, never by a count of listed strikes: three strikes out is 600 on BTC and 30 on ETH. (3) **Autopilot walks XAUT** (`AUTOPILOT_ASSETS`); the recorded playbooks and the proposals keep to BTC and ETH. (4) **Gold keeps the evening window**, which for it is the hour after one expiry settles; on a Saturday evening it has no next-day expiry and waits, on a Sunday evening it enters for Monday. (5) **The study scripts take the settlement hour from the coin**; the fetch scripts too. (6) The XAUT set is on paper as a check of a thin result, to be dropped if the paper record stays thin.
**Consequences.** The catalogue has 54 playbooks; the screen's coin filter shows ETH and XAUT rows of their own. Every figure is in-sample on one period. A strategy name is still held under the 80-character limit by a test (GAPS #153). Traceability HC-TR-235; GAPS #154.

## ADR-120 · Take profit in steps: a ladder of profit steps on any paper trade, and on Autopilot (2026-11-22)
**Context.** The owner watched profitable strangles give their profit back on a sudden move and proposed closing the position in parts: some lots at a first profit level, more at the next, the last part held; then asked for it to be configurable (three, four or five parts, early or late levels) with a good screen. Backtested first (`results-scale-out-2026-11-22.txt`; the strategy lab's `study_exits.py` tables): a four-part ladder at 25 / 50 / 75 % with a stop at triple earned about what closing everything at half does on BTC daily (+8.88 a day against +9.02) with a smaller worst day (-50.5 against -69), about the same on ETH daily (+3.15 against +3.10) and more on the BTC Friday weekly (+33.25 against +28.59); it earns less than the stop alone on all three. The lab also found that a ladder judged on the whole trade's realised plus open profit can never reach its last steps (the parts already banked hold the total down), so each step has to be judged on the lots still open.
**Decision.** (1) **A step is one rule** of a new kind `step` (trigger `pct`): it fires when the lots still open are up by its level, measured as their gain over the premium they were opened for (`openProfitPct`), and closes `closeShare` of every open leg in whole lots, never none and never the last one (`stepLots`). A ladder is one to four steps and a last part with no rule, held to a stop or to settlement. A step needs a premium worth a share of: the net of the open legs must be at least a cent and at least a twentieth of what the legs cost in all (`stepPremiumOk`), or legs that nearly cancel out (a risk reversal) would read a one-dollar move as half the premium; the route and the dialog refuse such a position and the engine waits on one that became so. A step that would close nothing, every leg being down to its last lot, is disarmed with the reason, never marked taken. A share of what the leg holds when the step fires, not a fixed lot count, so legs of different sizes give up the same proportion and a step still means something after a stop took a leg. (2) **Steps or a target, never both**; a stop, leg stops, the spot and time rules stay as they are and keep watching what is left. A step fire does not disarm the other rules. Steps are judged lowest level first and after every protective kind. (3) **Paper only for now**: the route refuses steps on a live trade and the engine holds one that went live with steps armed. The partial close reuses `closeLegRow`, which already splits a leg into a closed copy and the rest; live partial exits need their own checks (order sizing, a refused part) before this opens. (4) **The dialog** has a "Take profit in steps" box under the Target: presets Early 10 / 25 / 40 / 55, Standard 25 / 50 / 75 (the default) and Late 50 / 75, each with the last part held, and Custom rows of a level and whole lots; it shows what each step banks and refuses lots that do not add up, levels that do not rise and a level the open lots have already reached. The card reads "profit steps 1 of 4 taken, next at 50 %". It counts the steps armed together only, and says nothing once no step is armed. (5) **Autopilot**: a clock playbook may carry `ladder` (its levels; equal parts), and three new paper playbooks use 25 / 50 / 75 with a stop at triple beside the existing ones, which are unchanged: BTC daily, ETH daily, BTC Friday weekly.
**Consequences.** One column (`strategy_rules.close_share`, migration 0030); a rule set holds up to 19 rules. The backtest counts each quarter as its own small trade carrying its share of a stopped option's loss, while the engine measures the open lots only, so after a stop the steps come sooner here than in the figures. Realised profit is booked to the cent for each closed part, so a ladder's total can differ from one close by a cent a part. Traceability HC-TR-236 to HC-TR-239; GAPS #155.

## ADR-121 · Ten more Autopilot paper strategies from the strategy lab: a profit lock, a timed exit, half then trail, closer strikes, ETH wings, an afternoon entry (2026-11-22)
**Context.** The strategy lab (the research thread, `docs/research/STRATEGY-LAB.md` on branch `research/exits`) tested exits and entries the same day and reported to the owner, who approved each as a new paper strategy of its own beside the existing ones, none of which changes; the owner also approved three daily "bundles" by risk appetite (cautious: out by 1:30 AM; balanced: half then trail; bold: strikes half as far), whose tie to subscription plans is a later decision. Figures (100 lots a leg, the exchange's real fees, 106 daily expiries of 8 Aug to 21 Nov 2026 on 5-minute marks, 51 Friday expiries on hourly marks; each checked against the lab's tables before it was written on a card): a profit lock on BTC daily +11.68 a day (71 % of days, worst -69.2) against +11.75 (65 %, worst -106) for the stop alone, and on the BTC Friday weekly +40.95 a week (71 %) against +40.65 (57 %); out by 01:30 IST +6.18 on BTC (78 %, worst -25) and +2.58 on ETH (82 %, worst -19.9); half then trail with a 20 % give-back +11.30 on BTC (79 %, worst -69.2) and +3.65 on ETH; strikes 0.5 % away +14.60 on BTC (62 %, worst -114.4) and +6.84 on ETH; ETH sold 1 % away with wings at 3 %, held with no stop, +4.56 (73 % of 105 days, worst -47); a BTC entry two hours before settlement, 0.25 % away, +2.29 (75 %, worst -21.7), weak in the first half of the period and absent on ETH.
**Decision.** (1) **A profit lock is a rule of its own kind `lock`**: nothing happens until the strategy's profit (realised plus open) reaches its level; that tick writes the profit seen and exits nothing; from then on the whole strategy is closed if the profit is back at a fixed floor. The floor does not rise with the profit (that is a trailing target, ADR-103). It is armed by Autopilot only: a body cannot carry one, the dialog's save leaves it in place, and "Disarm all" removes it. Judged after the protective kinds and before a step or a target; it books the reason `target`. (2) **A clock playbook gains three optional exits**: `lock` (shares of the premium for the level and the floor), `exitHourUtc` (a time rule at the first such hour after the entry) and `takeTrailPct` (the take level starts a trail with that give-back instead of closing: a trailing target). (3) **Ten playbooks** (`LAB_DAILY_PLAYBOOKS`, `LAB_WEEKLY_PLAYBOOKS`): the lock on BTC daily and the BTC Friday weekly; out by 01:30 IST on BTC and ETH; half then trail on BTC and ETH; 0.5 % away on BTC and ETH; ETH with wings at 3 % and no stop; the BTC afternoon entry (window 10:00 to 11:00 UTC, the expiry that settles two hours later) with its card saying it is one to watch. The lock is on BTC only: on ETH it earned less than the stop alone. (4) No bundle is built: the three strategies they name exist as rows, and how a plan selects them is undecided.
**Consequences.** The catalogue has 67 playbooks. No migration (the kind is a text column). Every figure is in-sample on one period and judged on bar closes; the lab's model of the lock counts profit before the entry fee, as the engine does. The lab's tables are on `research/exits`, not yet on main. Traceability HC-TR-240, HC-TR-241; GAPS #156.

## ADR-122 · One side after a move: two Autopilot paper strategies that sell only the side behind a 24-hour move (2026-11-22)
**Context.** The strategy lab's trend table (`results-trend-2026-11-22.txt` on branch `research/exits`: four look-backs, three thresholds and three rules on BTC and ETH daily, 72 rows, stop at triple, strikes 1 % away, the 21:30 IST entry, 100 lots, the exchange's real fees) judged each side of the evening strangle alone, grouped by the spot's move before the entry. In all 24 cells the rule selling only the side behind the move beat the rule selling only the side ahead of it (the side ahead still earned on most days; it carried the worst ones). The plain choice, the move over the 24 hours before the entry beyond 1 %, selling only the side behind it and both sides otherwise: BTC +10.73 a day (72 % of 105 days positive, worst -73.4) against +11.76 (65 %, worst -105.8) for both sides always on the same days; ETH +3.91 (69 %, worst -40.1) against +4.42 (61 %, worst -49.6). A little less income, more winning days, a smaller worst day; ETH weaker in the second half of the period. The owner approved it as a new paper strategy on BTC and ETH daily, beside the existing ones, and asked that it be watched closely.
**Decision.** (1) **A clock playbook may carry `moveSide { hours, pct }`.** At entry the spot of the snapshot being priced is compared with the spot `hours` earlier: up by more than `pct` per cent the proposal keeps only the put, down by more than that only the call, otherwise the whole structure (`sideAfterMove`, `proposalFor`). The premium, the extremes, the structure key and the rules follow from the legs kept: one sold option has one stop. (2) **The earlier spot is the stored one**: the `iv_snapshots` row nearest that instant, within 30 minutes either side (`spotNear`, `SPOT_NEAR_MS`); it is read only while the entry window is open. **No such row, no entry**: the pass is skipped with "No spot price from 24 hours ago to judge the move on". Selling both sides on such an evening was rejected: the study left those days out, so it would be an untested trade under a tested name. (3) **Two playbooks** (`MOVE_DAILY_PLAYBOOKS`): `evening-strangle-one-side` (BTC) and `eth-evening-strangle-one-side` (ETH), 24 hours, 1 %, strikes 1 % away, stop at triple, held to settlement. (4) **The entry records the move** (per cent, signed) and the side in the run's detail, and the log line shows it, so the paper record can be split by side. `docs/research/scripts/autopilot-study/one-side-monitor.sql` lists each trade beside the evening strangle with a stop at triple of the same coin and day; on ETH that is the study's comparison (1 % away), on BTC it is not (that playbook sells three strikes out, about 0.7 %), so the BTC columns show the day, not a like-for-like difference.
**Consequences.** The catalogue has 69 playbooks. No migration. Paper only, as every Autopilot trade. The study measured the move on the exchange's candles and the engine on its own five-minute snapshots: the two can disagree when the move is within a few hundredths of 1 %. A server that was down 24 hours earlier skips that evening. One sold option has nothing on the other side to offset it. The figures are in-sample on one period in which moves continued more often than they reversed; in a ranging market the opposite could hold. Traceability HC-TR-242, HC-TR-243; GAPS #157.

## ADR-123 · Buy back when cheap: a sold option bought back on its own once its price has fallen to a level (2026-11-22)
**Context.** On 22 Nov 2026 the owner watched a paper evening strangle on its expiry day: a call sold at 421.7 fell to about 5 dollars at 13:33 IST, then BTC ran through its strike and the call's mark reached about 686 two hours later (its stop at double, 843, was not reached). At 5 there was about 1 % of the option's premium left to earn. The owner asked for such legs to be handled: buy the option back at a price, in dollars, not at a share of its entry. The strategy lab tested it the same day (`study_cheap.py`, `results-cheap-leg-2026-11-22.txt` on `research/exits`; BTC daily 1 % away with a stop at triple, 106 expiries, 5-minute marks, 100 lots, the buy-back's fee counted): at a mark of 5, +11.30 a day (66 % of days positive, worst -106.3) against +11.75 (65 %, worst -105.8) for holding. 163 of 212 sold options fell to 5 (median 15.4 hours in); of those 17 later rose above 25 and 9 above 50, 2 went on to their stop, none settled above its entry price and 2 settled with any value; the highest later mark was 184.4, so the owner's day is outside the sample. No fixed price (20, 10, 5, 2 dollars) beat holding on BTC daily. Of the 40 rows (those four prices and 20, 10, 5, 2 % of entry, on five sets) four did beat their hold row: 20 % of entry on BTC daily (+13.34 against +11.75, 73 % of days, worst -111.4) and on BTC daily with the stop at double (+9.91 against +7.58), 5 % of entry on the BTC weekly (+43.04 against +40.65) and a mark of 0.1 on the ETH weekly (+14.54 against +14.43). A buy-back at 20 % of entry is an early profit take on one side, a different rule from the one the owner asked for; it is noted here as the row worth a look, not built. It is protection against a rare comeback, paid for a little every day; the owner approved it on that footing, as a rule and as one new paper strategy.
**Decision.** (1) **A rule kind `leg_take`** ("Leg buy-back"): trigger `price`, one per leg, always scope leg. It watches one sold option and fires when that option's mark is at or below the level; that option alone is bought back and booked with the reason `target`; nothing else is disarmed, and the stop that watched the same option becomes void on the next tick, as for any closed leg. If it was the last open leg the trade is archived with `target`, or with `stopped` when another leg of it was stopped. A mark that is not above zero is no mark: the rule fires downwards, so a venue's placeholder for a missing price must not read as a fall to the level. Judged after the protective kinds and the lock, before a step or a target. (2) **Paper only for now.** The route refuses it on a live trade (409) and the engine holds one that reaches a live trade ("not sent: a buy-back of a cheap leg is on paper trades only for now"): an option marked at a few dollars often cannot be bought at its mark, and a live version needs a limit order and its own checks. (3) **The route accepts it only for an open sold option, at a level below the price it was sold at**; the dialog also refuses a level the mark is already at or under. (4) **The Protect dialog** has a "Buy back when cheap" section on a paper trade, one row per sold option, 5 as the starting price. (5) **A clock playbook may carry `legBuyBackAt`** (a mark); Autopilot arms one buy-back per sold option whose entry price is above it. **One playbook** (`BUYBACK_DAILY_PLAYBOOKS`): `evening-strangle-buy-back`, BTC daily, 1 % away, stop at triple, buy-back at 5 dollars. A fixed 5 dollars as the owner asked, on BTC only: on ETH daily the matching levels also earned less than holding. The level is a price in the coin's quote money, so it means different things at different premiums; a share of the entry price was tested and rejected by the owner as not what was meant.
**Consequences.** The catalogue has 70 playbooks; a strategy may carry up to 29 rules (ten leg stops, ten leg buy-backs, five others, four steps). No migration (the kind is a text column). The paper fill is at the mark, which flatters the rule. The card says protection, not income, and that the day that prompted it is outside the backtest. Traceability HC-TR-244, HC-TR-245, HC-TR-246; GAPS #158.

## ADR-124 · The buy-back level as a share of the price sold at, and a paper strategy at 20 % (2026-11-22)
**Context.** ADR-123 built the buy-back of a cheap option with the level as a price, as the owner asked, and recorded that one row of the lab's table (`results-cheap-leg-2026-11-22.txt`, `research/exits` 240ced5) did beat holding on BTC daily: a buy-back at 20 % of the entry price, +13.34 a day (73 % of 106 days positive, worst -111.4, halves +753 / +661) against +11.75 (65 %, worst -105.8), and +9.91 against +7.58 with the stop at double. Against it, from the same table: at 10 % of entry BTC daily earned +11.12, less than holding, so the gain is not smooth in the level; at 20 % ETH daily earned +2.84 against +4.46, the BTC weekly +34.59 against +40.65 and the ETH weekly +9.88 against +14.43. 182 of 212 sold options fell to 20 % (median 9.9 hours in); 9 of those would later have reached their stop and 8 settled above their entry. Told this, the owner asked for both ways of giving the level, a price or a percentage, and for the 20 % version as a paper strategy.
**Decision.** (1) **A `leg_take` rule also takes the trigger `multiple`**: a share of the leg's entry price, above 0 and below 1 (0.2 is 20 %), priced into the stored level when the rule is armed, exactly as a leg stop's multiple is. The engine is unchanged: it compares the mark with the stored level. The route refuses a share with no entry price yet, a level that rounds to nothing and a level at or above the price sold at. (2) **The Protect dialog** offers "price" or "% of sold" on each buy-back row, shows the price a percentage stands for, and the card line reads "bought back at 180.00 (20 % of the price sold at)". A percentage is a multiple on the wire; the trader never types 0.2. (3) **A clock playbook may carry `legBuyBackShare`** (one buy-back a leg: a plan that also has a price arms the price); Autopilot arms each sold option's buy-back at that share of its own price. **One playbook** (`BUYBACK_SHARE_PLAYBOOKS`): `evening-strangle-buy-back-20pct`, BTC daily, 1 % away, stop at triple. BTC daily only: the row helped on the two BTC daily sets (stop at triple, stop at double) and on none of the other three. Its card and its trials note say it is one good cell among poor ones and to expect less than the figure.
**Consequences.** The catalogue has 71 playbooks. No migration. Paper only, as ADR-123. At 20 % the option is far from worthless (a fifth of its premium, tens of dollars on BTC), so the paper fill at the mark is closer to a real one than at 5 dollars, but still a mark. Traceability HC-TR-247, HC-TR-248; GAPS #159.

## ADR-125 · The BTC Friday weekly closed at 90 % of the premium, as a paper strategy (2026-11-22)
**Context.** Two of the strategy lab's tables of the day (`results-cheap-leg-2026-11-22.txt`, 240ced5, and `results-tight-2026-11-22.txt`, 3b298f4, on `research/exits`) close the whole trade at a share of the premium on the BTC Friday weekly (51 expiries, 3 % away, stop at triple, hourly marks, 100 lots, real fees). Against +40.65 a week for holding to settlement (57 % of weeks positive, worst -168.3, halves +1525 / +548): at 75 % +43.58 (65 %), at 90 % +44.39 (59 %, worst -168.3, halves +1524 / +740), at 95 % +43.16 (59 %, halves +1502 / +699), at 98 % +41.28, at 99 % +40.26. On the other sets the 90 % close was behind holding on BTC daily (+10.99 against +11.75), level with the stop at double (+7.56 against +7.58), and slightly ahead on ETH daily (+4.57 against +4.46) and on the ETH Friday weekly (+15.10 against +14.43, worst week the same). The lab first proposed 95 %; the owner asked whether the existing option could do it, approved it as a new strategy beside the weekly ones, and then chose 90 % instead of 95 %, which is also the best row.
**Decision.** **One playbook** (`TAKE90_WEEKLY_PLAYBOOKS`): `weekly-fri-strangle-take-90`, BTC, the same Friday window, expiry and strikes as the plain weekly with a stop at triple, with `takeProfitOfCredit: 0.9`. No new rule and no engine change: a clock playbook's take-profit is a share of the premium collected (ADR-113), armed as a strategy target in USD. BTC weekly only, as the owner asked: the BTC daily sets do not support it, and the ETH rows, slightly ahead, are noted and not built. The 95 % version was built on the branch and replaced before it was committed; it does not exist.
**Consequences.** The catalogue has 72 playbooks. No migration. Paper only. The edge is small, in-sample on one year of weekly trades, came in the second half of that year, and is judged on hourly closes; the engine judges the target on live marks every two seconds, so it will take the 90 % at a different moment than the study did, usually earlier. The target is on the profit before the exit fee. Traceability HC-TR-249; GAPS #160.

## ADR-126 · The Paper list read by coin and by who opened the trade (2026-11-23)
**Context.** With Autopilot on, the owner's Paper tab held 55 open and 132 closed trades in one list of ten a page. The owner asked to tell Autopilot's trades from their own, to see when a trade was entered and exited, and to filter by coin. The card already carried an Autopilot tag (ADR-109) and the entry and exit times (ADR-105); what was missing was a way to narrow the list, and a daily trade read "0d" for its length.
**Decision.** Two chip groups beside the status chips: Coin (All and each coin the tab has traded) and Opened by (All, Autopilot, By me; Paper tab only, since an Autopilot trade never goes live, ADR-108). A group is offered only when it would split the tab. Every chip, the status chips included, counts what it would show under the other filters. The card says "open for" or "held" with minutes, hours or days in place of whole days. The closed list has a sort of its own, which starts on the date and there reads the exit time, newest first; the open lists keep the trader's sort, and neither changes the other. A line above the list, shown on Closed, under any filter or with a search typed, names what the list is narrowed to (coin, opener, account, search) and adds up the trades listed: how many, how many ended above zero (closed only) and their total, marked "before fees" on both tabs: the realised figure is price difference times size on paper and on live alike, and no fee is taken off it. A filter whose value has gone (the coin's last trade deleted, the key removed) stops filtering. The strip of five tiles above stays the open book, unfiltered, as before.
**Rejected.** A separate tab for Autopilot trades: it splits one list in two and loses the combined view. Renaming "started / closed" to "entered / exited" on the card: the pane and Details say started and closed, and one wording across the three is clearer than a better word in one place.
**Consequences.** No API, schema or database change. The filters are view state of the tab: a reload returns to All. The Desk's pixel baselines move (the filter row), so they need the CI refresh after the merge. Traceability HC-TR-250, HC-TR-251, HC-TR-252; GAPS #161.

## ADR-127 · Fees, the result after fees, capital and one overall figure on the Paper and Live lists (2026-11-23)
**Context.** The owner asked for the capital a trade needs, its fees and the profit after them on every card, the same on Live, and one answer to "what is my overall profit or loss". The cards showed P&L before fees only; the strip's Total P&L is the open book; fees appeared only on the Autopilot results screen, as an estimate made at entry (entry fill times two).
**Decision.** (1) `tradeFees` in the pricing package estimates a whole trade's fees from its stored legs under the venue's published rule (ADR-099): each entry fill, each exit made, the settlement fee of a leg that expired in the money, nothing for one that expired worthless. A stored leg does not carry the spot at its fill, so the option's strike stands in for the rule's notional (an option that settled in the money is the exception: its spot is its strike and its value); every figure is marked "est.". (2) Each card gains a money line: capital, fees, after fees, and the profit after fees as a share of the capital. The big figure stays before fees and now says so. (3) Capital is the worst loss at expiry of the position at its entry premiums, the figure the Builder already calls its margin estimate; a closed trade is read on every leg it entered. A position with more options sold than bought on its call side or on its put side is not read that way (a sold call has no worst loss, and a sold put's loss down to a price of zero is not what it ties up): the card says "sold without cover", a futures leg says "futures margin", and neither shows a capital figure or a return. The capital comes straight from the legs (the exact extremes of the expiry payoff), so a closed card needs no price and no worker. An open card that has closed some legs shows no return: its capital is what is still held and its P&L carries what was closed. (4) The summary line gives before fees, fees and after fees, and a closed trade has won only when it is above zero after its fees. (5) A tile "Overall result" adds closed and open trades after fees and follows the coin, opener and account chips. The same on the Live tab, with the same estimates.
**Why no margin figure for uncovered sold options.** The plan was to pass the exchange's margin percent through the public instrument list and apply the live preview's estimate (ADR-091). The exchange's public product list, read on 23 Nov 2026 (the first 500 of its 1201 option products), gives a different `initial_margin` per contract by time to expiry (BTC options: 0.5 on the 23 and 24 Nov expiries, 1 from 25 Nov to 25 Dec, 5 on the 29 Jan monthly; ETH 1 and, on 29 Jan, 5; XAUT 1 on the three expiries in the sample), and a contract leaves the list when it expires. A figure computed on read would therefore be wrong for a trade entered days ago and impossible for a closed one. The estimate has to be recorded when the trade is entered, which needs a column and the three entry paths (paper, live, Autopilot), and it has never been compared with what Delta blocks (GAPS #109). Not built here; GAPS #162.
**Rejected.** A tiered rule in the code that guesses the percent from days to expiry: it would be an invented exchange parameter. Charging an exit fee on open trades in advance: the card says what has been paid so far.
**Consequences.** No API, schema or database change. Cards and the Autopilot results screen estimate fees differently (strike against entry spot; exits as they happened against a full exit assumed), so their totals differ by small amounts. Traceability HC-TR-253 to HC-TR-256; GAPS #162.

## ADR-128 · The margin of a trade is estimated once, when it is entered, and stored with it (2026-11-23)
**Context.** ADR-127 left sold options without cover, which is most Autopilot trades, with no capital figure: the exchange lists an initial margin percent per contract, the percent changes as the contract nears its expiry, and the contract leaves the public list when it expires, so the figure cannot be worked out on read. The owner approved recording it at entry.
**Decision.** (1) The venue's public product record carries `initial_margin` and `initial_margin_scaling_factor`; the Delta REST parser keeps both on the venue instrument when listed (`initialMarginPct`, `initialMarginScalingFactor`). (2) `apps/api/src/margin.ts` estimates a trade's margin with the live preview's arithmetic (ADR-091, ADR-095): every sold option under `shortOptionMarginUsd`, every futures leg under `futuresMarginUsd`, plus the net premium paid; no relief for a bought option on the same side. It returns nothing when the trade has nothing margined, and nothing when any margined leg cannot be estimated, so a part figure is never stored. The public list this API reads holds options only, so a paper trade with a futures leg finds no parameters for that leg and gets no figure; a live one takes the preview's, which reads the product itself. (3) `strategies.margin_estimate` (migration 0031, nullable) is written at the three entries: the paper start route (the venue's spot and the entry prices), the live placement and the batch placement (the preview's own `marginRequired`, the figure the placement was checked against), and Autopilot's entry (the snapshot's spot and marks). The product list is read through the venue clients and kept for a minute, since an Autopilot pass enters dozens of trades at once; a caller that holds the spot (Autopilot's snapshot) hands it over and no ticker is read; a data-only venue is asked nothing. The paper start reads before it writes anything and waits three seconds at most. A venue that is slow or does not answer never fails an entry: the trade is stored without a figure. A trade stopped or restored to a draft loses its figure. (4) The card of a trade sold without cover, or with a futures leg, shows the stored figure as its capital, marked "min. margin est.", and the return "on est. margin"; the hint says it is the exchange's listed minimum for the trade as entered, that the exchange may hold more, and that it is not updated when a leg is closed; without one it says "sold without cover" as before. A covered trade keeps its worst loss as capital.
**What the figure is not.** It has never been compared with what Delta blocks (GAPS #109: the unit of the scaling factor is unverified, and whether a strangle's two sides get relief is unknown). One run by hand on 23 Nov 2026 against the exchange's public list (a script of this session, not in the repo, so not reproducible from it), BTC spot 61,528, a strangle sold 100 lots a side: the same day's expiry, 61,000 put and 62,200 call at marks of 70.8 and 116.7, percent 0.5: 80.30 USD; the next day's, the same strikes at 537.2 and 612.3, percent 0.5: 176.51 USD; the 29 Jan monthly, 61,000 put and 62,000 call at 4,987 and 4,432, percent 5: 1,557.23 USD. Returns on such small capital read large; if the exchange holds more than the listed minimum, the card overstates them.
**Rejected.** Estimating on read from today's list (wrong for an older trade, impossible for a closed one). A rule in the code that guesses the percent from days to expiry (an invented exchange parameter). Back-filling the trades already in the database (their contracts' percents are gone).
**Consequences.** Migration 0031, additive. The strategy shape the web parses is strict, and the API now sends the field on every row: the web must be deployed with or before the API, and a tab or dev server still on the old schema fails to read the strategies list until it reloads (the GAPS #153 kind). Trades entered before it have no figure and never will. The estimate is not refreshed when a trade is adjusted. Traceability HC-TR-257; GAPS #163.

## ADR-129 · The daily strangle closed at 90 % of the premium or trailed from it, BTC and ETH, as paper strategies (2026-11-23)
**Context.** The owner watched paper trades give profit back near the end ("because of wild moves running in profit trade can decrease") and approved, with the strategy lab, closing the daily strangle at 90 % of the premium on BTC and ETH. Asked to confirm in the build thread they asked "why don't we trail" instead, and the lab ran it (`results-trail-from-90-2026-11-23.txt`, `study_trail90.py`, on `research/exits`, uncommitted there when this was written and pushed since as fc7bb98): 106 daily expiries a coin, 1 % away, stop at triple, 5-minute marks, real fees. BTC: held +11.75 a day (65 % of days, worst -105.8); closed at 90 % +10.99 (66 %); trailed from 90 % with a give-back of 5 % of the best +11.23, of 10 % +11.05, of 20 % +10.67; the worst day and the drawdown (-185.0) the same in every row. ETH: held +4.46 (61 %, worst -49.6); closed at 90 % +4.57 (62 %); trailed 5 / 10 / 20 % +4.22 / +4.25 / +4.26. Of the trades that reached 90 % (BTC 67, ETH 57) and were held on, BTC 60 ended higher (mean +3.30) and 7 lower (mean -16.79), together +80.62; ETH 51 higher (mean +1.76) and 6 lower (mean -16.89), together -11.34. The study fills an exit at the first 5-minute close at or under its level; that slip moves a trail row by 0.3 to 0.6 a trade (filled at the level: BTC 5 % +11.56, ETH 5 % +4.66), more than any difference between closing and the trails; holding has no such slip, and its lead over closing on BTC (0.76 a day) is larger than the slip but too small to tell apart on 106 days; the order flips between the coins. The lab's trail grid of the same day (`results-trail-grid-2026-11-23.txt`, arm 40 to 75 %, give-back 10 to 40 %) and its stepped lock (`results-stepped-lock-2026-11-23.txt`) found nothing better than what is built and are not built.
**Decision.** **Four playbooks** (`TAKE90_DAILY_PLAYBOOKS`), beside the existing ones, on the owner's "go" for all four: `evening-strangle-take-90` and `eth-evening-strangle-take-90` (`takeProfitOfCredit: 0.9`), `evening-strangle-trail-90` and `eth-evening-strangle-trail-90` (`takeProfitOfCredit: 0.9`, `takeTrailPct: 5`). The evening window, expiry, strikes (1 % away) and stop at triple are the daily strangle's. No new rule and no engine change: the take-profit of a clock playbook is a share of the premium armed as a strategy target (ADR-113), and with a trail percentage the target trails instead of closing (ADR-103, ADR-121). The give-back is 5 %, the tightest: the best trail row on BTC, and on ETH level with the other two (the lowest of the three by 0.04); at 90 % its exit level is 85.5 % of the premium. They are built as a comparison, not on an edge: the history cannot separate closing, trailing and holding, and the paper record on live marks is what will. The card of each says so, the rows behind holding included. The weekly card's sentence that the daily rule was not built is corrected; that strategy is unchanged.
**Rejected.** Building only the two rows the study leaned to (the trail on BTC, the close on ETH): the lean is inside the slip, and a pair on each coin is the comparison the owner asked for. A give-back of 10 or 20 %: behind 5 % on BTC, level on ETH, and an exit near 81 % or 72 % is not what a rule armed at 90 % is for. A trail in place of the weekly's close at 90 % (ADR-125): existing strategies are not changed.
**Consequences.** The catalogue has 76 playbooks (36 daily). No migration. Paper only. The owner switches the four on in the Autopilot screen; nothing is enabled by the build. The engine judges the level on live marks every two seconds, so both rules will act at other moments than the study did. Both levels are on the profit before every fee: the paper P&L the engine judges carries none, and the study adds the entry fee back before it compares (`study_trail90.py`). The trail's band is narrow: at the mean premium of the study (BTC 36.5, ETH 21.0) a give-back of 5 % of the best is about 1.6 and 1 USD, about the size of the study's own slip, so on two-second marks the trail may fire soon after it starts and read as a close a little under 90 %; a trailing target has no least give-back. Once the trail runs each option is near a tenth of its price, so the stops at triple no longer matter. Found by the review and older than this change: the rules tick passes a venue mark through as it comes (`apps/api/src/venues.ts`, the `tick` source), so a sold option's mark arriving as 0 would read as full profit to a strategy target, which a leg buy-back guards against and a target does not; these four act where marks are cents. Closing at 90 % or on a trail buys back two cheap options at their marks, which a real exchange may not fill at. Traceability HC-TR-258; GAPS #164.

## ADR-130 · The BTC daily strangle with the profit lock and the buy-back at 5 dollars together, as a paper strategy (2026-11-23)
**Context.** The lab's table of exit combinations (`results-combo-2026-11-22.txt`, 9daa8fd on `research/exits`; 28 rows, 11 on BTC daily: 106 expiries, 1 % away, stop at triple, real fees) puts two exits that are already built on one trade. On BTC daily: held +11.75 a day (65 % of days, worst -105.8); the lock 50 > 25 alone +11.68 (71 %, worst -69.2); the buy-back at 5 dollars alone +11.30 (66 %, worst -106.3); both together +11.34 (71 %, worst -69.6, worst three -160.4, halves +655 / +547). No combination in the table beat its better part; the lock with a buy-back at 20 % of the price sold at earned +12.66, under that buy-back alone (+13.34). The owner approved the lock with the buy-back at 5 with the lab ("Approve the buy-back with profit lock on BTC daily as a paper strategy") and confirmed it in the build thread.
**Decision.** **One playbook** (`COMBO_DAILY_PLAYBOOKS`): `evening-strangle-lock-buy-back`, BTC, the daily strangle's window, expiry and strikes, with `legStop: 3`, `lock: { at: 0.5, floor: 0.25 }` and `legBuyBackAt: 5`: the same lock as `evening-strangle-lock` (ADR-121) and the same buy-back as `evening-strangle-buy-back` (ADR-123). No new rule and no engine change: a clock plan already carries both and the entry arms each from its own field. The lock is judged on the whole trade, realised and open together, so what a buy-back has banked counts toward it; that is how the table ran it and what the engine does (the total a rule is judged on is the realised total plus the open legs at their marks), and the API test drives it: the bought-back call alone leaves the trade under half the premium, a small gain on the put then sets the lock, and the trade closes when the whole profit is back at the floor. It is built for the two protections together, not for income: it is 0.34 a day under the lock alone, and the card says so and names the 20 % row that earned more and was not chosen.
**Rejected.** The lock with the buy-back at 20 % of the price sold at (the better row): the owner asked for 5 dollars; it can be added beside this one if wanted. Changing `evening-strangle-lock` to carry the buy-back: existing strategies are not changed.
**Consequences.** The catalogue has 77 playbooks (37 daily). No migration. Paper only; the owner switches it on in the Autopilot screen.
