A buy-back at 5 banks nearly half the premium by itself, so the lock is usually set soon after it (as soon as the other option has gained about 5 dollars of price), and the other option is then closed when the whole profit falls to the floor, well before its stop at triple: with two options sold at P each, at about 1.5 P. Only in the short time between a buy-back and the lock being set is the other option on its stop alone. This is how the study ran it. A lock that fires closes what is open and the trade is booked with the reason `target` as any lock is, also when a stopped option has left the whole trade at a loss (the correction to `stopped` is made for a buy-back only; older than this change). The row of a running trade on the Autopilot screen reads no lock and no buy-back and says "no level: held to settlement" for this trade, as it does for the two it combines; the card's longer text has no sentence for the buy-back (it is in the summary and the one-line rule). The case the buy-back is for (22 Nov, an option at 5 that came back through its strike) is outside the 106 days. Traceability HC-TR-259; GAPS #165.

## ADR-131 · Once a running trade has booked a result, its payoff figures are the whole trade's (2026-11-23)
**Context.** The owner opened the Details of a BTC Friday weekly strangle whose sold call had been stopped (realised -17,145.89 INR) and read "Max profit +9,279.77" and "BE 57,389" under a total of -9,711.54: "How will we get the profit around 9200 from this trade, when we already hit the stop loss on the other leg?" The figures were right for the open put alone (its premium, its own break-even) and wrong as an answer about the trade: the best it could end at was about -7,866. The card's "max profit" and "POP", the Details payoff line and chart, and the analysis pane following the trade all priced only the legs still open (ADR-044 decision 4, HC-TR-119, HC-TR-143), which is the whole trade only until a leg is closed.
**Decision.** A running trade (paper or live) whose realised total is not zero is priced with a **booked pair** beside its open legs (`bookedOf` in `apps/web/src/lib/strategy/paper.ts`): one lot of a future bought and one sold, their prices apart by exactly the realised total. The pair is worth that amount at every price and date, has no delta and no theta, and the engine counts no premium for a future, so everything the engine derives from the payoff (max profit, max loss, break-evens, the curve, the probability of profit, reward to risk, the scenario grid) is the whole trade's, while the net premium and the greeks stay the open legs' own. It is the workbench's own device for a draft's exits (ADR-094), applied to exits already made. The result is marked (`withBooked`, `BookedResult.booked`) so the margin estimate leaves the booked amount out: margin is what the legs still held tie up. Where it shows: the card (`CardFigures`: "incl. X realised"; "best case" in place of "max profit" when even the best case is a loss), Details (the payoff line says "whole trade · incl. X realised", the mini chart and the POP tile follow), and the pane following the trade (a line above the tiles, "Best case" on the tile, no Max ROI). The word is "realised", the one the Details tile already uses for the same figure. In the adjustment workbench every figure carries it: the before and after strip, the footer, the plan rows and the repair ideas (`useDraftFigures`), so they describe the same whole trade and their comparison is unchanged. Two places must not carry it and do not: the worst loss sent to the venue's balance check with a live adjust preview, and the "at risk" line of the pause before it, are the held legs' own (`heldWorstLoss`), because what is realised is no risk left to cover; this was the one path where the pair would have left the browser, found by the review before it was committed. The Share dialog shows the whole trade's figures with a note that the link carries the open legs only. The realised total is the server's own figure, before fees, like the P&L beside it. A closed trade is untouched (it is already drawn on every leg it traded), as are drafts, the Builder and the assistant's summary (both read the Builder's own legs), exchange positions, the portfolio strip and the bases of stops in the Protect dialog.
**Rejected.** An offset option in the pricing engine: the exact extremes and break-evens are solved from the legs, so a constant would need its own path through each of them, where a pair gets all of it from the code that exists. Relabelling the figures "open legs only" and leaving them: true, and still not the answer to what the trader asks. Summing the closed legs' own entry and exit prices: misses a partial exit on a leg still open; the realised total has it.
**Consequences.** Web only: no API, schema or pricing change, no migration. A trade with nothing booked reads exactly as before. A trade that has booked a profit shows a higher best and worst case than its open legs alone. The capital on the card's money line is unchanged (what is still held), so on a card with a booked loss the max loss and the capital no longer mirror each other. The pane shows no Max ROI for such a trade (the whole trade's best case over the open legs' margin is no ratio). Pixel baselines move only for trades with a booked result. Traceability HC-TR-260; GAPS #166.

## ADR-132 · The daily strangle trailed from 15 % of the premium, on BTC and ETH, as paper strategies; Gold held back (2026-11-23)
**Context.** The owner asked the strategy lab for an early, small take with a trail ("deploy with 15% profit and trail and exit"), chose the give-back ("Build the trail from 15 % with a 30 % give-back on BTC and ETH daily"), added Gold ("Add Gold daily to the trail from 15 % with a 30 % give-back") and confirmed each in the build thread. The lab's table for BTC and ETH: `results-trail-from-15-2026-11-23.txt` (c90d686 on `research/exits`; 106 daily expiries a coin, 1 % away, stop at triple, 5-minute marks, real fees; a trail started at 15 and 25 % with give-backs of 10, 20, 30 and 50 %, 16 rows). BTC: held +11.75 a day (65 % of days, worst -105.8, drawdown -185.0); 15 / 30: +4.21 (78 %, worst -37.5, drawdown -58.6, halves +243 / +204, 93 of 106 closed by the trail; filled at its level +6.07); 25 / 30: +6.34 (83 %, worst -50.5). ETH: held +4.46 (61 %, worst -49.6); 15 / 30: +2.97 (86 %, worst -44.6, drawdown -61.8, halves +134 / +180, 85 closed by the trail; at its level +3.73); 25 / 30: +3.07.
**Decision.** **Two playbooks**, beside the existing ones (`TRAIL15_DAILY_PLAYBOOKS`): `evening-strangle-trail-15` and `eth-evening-strangle-trail-15`, the daily strangle's window, expiry, strikes 1 % away and stop at triple, with `takeProfitOfCredit: 0.15`, `takeTrailPct: 30`. No new rule and no engine change: the trailing take-profit of ADR-113 / ADR-103, as in ADR-129. At 15 % the exit level starts at 10.5 % of the premium. The level and the give-back are the owner's; the cards say what they cost: about a third of holding on BTC, about two thirds on ETH where the worst day is no smaller, and that a start at 25 % earned more on BTC.
**Gold, built and taken out before the commit.** A Gold playbook (`xaut-evening-strangle-trail-15`, the Gold strangle of ADR-119 with its stop at double and the same take and trail) was built on the lab's first Gold table (`results-trail-from-15-XAUT-2026-11-23.txt`, aaa3c8a), which showed the trail level with holding (+0.10 against +0.09 a day). The code review found that table enters 23 hours before the settlement, and Autopilot's evening window enters Gold 24 hours before: its holding row is the 23-hour section of `results-daily-XAUT-2026-11-22.txt`, and the existing Gold cards are from the 24-hour section (holding with the stop at double +0.19). The owner, told this, said "commit without Gold". The lab's re-run at 24 hours (`results-trail-from-15-XAUT-24h-2026-11-23.txt`, uncommitted in its worktree when this was written; 91 trades, mean premium 1.3 USD) then gave, at the stop at double: held +0.19 (48 %, worst -2.1, drawdown -5.4); trail 15 / 30 +0.15 (79 %, worst -2.1, drawdown -2.5, 63 closed by the trail; filled at its level +0.23). So at the traded hour the trail costs about 0.04 a trade on Gold for less than half the drawdown, on amounts of cents where the slip is the larger part of the result. It is not built; it is the owner's to ask for again on those figures.
**Rejected.** A start at 25 % (more income on BTC in the table): not what the owner asked for; it can be added beside these.
**Consequences.** The catalogue has 79 playbooks (39 daily). No migration. Paper only; nothing is enabled by the build, and the first night they can enter is the one after the owner merges and switches them on. The engine judges the level on live marks every two seconds, so the trail will act at other moments than the study, usually nearer its level. A give-back of 30 % of a best of a few dollars is a narrow band in money, and a trailing target has no least give-back. The levels are on the profit before every fee, and at 15 % that matters more than anywhere else in the catalogue: under the exchange's fee cap (3.5 % of the premium a fill, plus GST) the fees of entering and leaving can be up to about 8 % of the premium, against a first exit level of 10.5 %. The study's figures are after fees; the paper P&L on the card is before them, and the card's money line is the one to read. Traceability HC-TR-261; GAPS #167.

## ADR-133 · The Gold daily strangle trailed from 15 % of the premium with a give-back of 20 %, as a paper strategy (2026-11-23)
**Context.** ADR-132 left Gold out: the table the owner had approved it on was entered an hour later than Autopilot enters Gold. The lab re-ran it at the traded hour (`results-trail-from-15-XAUT-24h-2026-11-23.txt`, 1c60acd on `research/exits`: 91 Gold expiries, 1 % away, entered 24 hours before the settlement, 5-minute marks, real fees, mean premium 1.3 USD; a trail started at 15, 25 and 50 % with give-backs of 10, 20, 30 and 50 %, at the stops at double and triple, 24 rows). With the stop at double: held +0.19 a day (48 % of days, total +17, worst -2.1, drawdown -5.4, halves +4 / +13); trail 15 / 20: +0.15 (82 %, total +14, worst -1.4, drawdown -1.4, halves +7 / +7, 68 closed by the trail, slip 4.6 points, filled at its level +0.18); 15 / 30: +0.15 (79 %, worst -2.1, drawdown -2.5); 15 / 10: +0.13; 50 / 10: +0.23 (67 %, worst -1.4). The owner, shown the 24-hour figures by the lab, chose: "Build Gold with the trail from 15 % and a 20 % give-back, stop at double", and confirmed it in the build thread.
**Decision.** **One playbook** (`TRAIL15_XAUT_PLAYBOOKS`): `xaut-evening-strangle-trail-15`, built on the Gold strangle of ADR-119 (its window, expiry, strikes 1 % away, stop at double) with `takeProfitOfCredit: 0.15`, `takeTrailPct: 20`. The give-back is 20 %, not the 30 % of BTC and ETH (ADR-132): at the same average it had the smaller worst day and drawdown. No new rule and no engine change. The card says it costs a little income against holding (about 0.04 a trade), that the money is cents, and that a trail started at 50 % earned more than holding and is not built. Its evidence is the 24-hour table only.
**Rejected.** The trail started at 50 % with a give-back of 10 % (the best row, +0.23): not what the owner asked for; it can be added beside this one. The first table (23 hours): the wrong hour, kept in the lab's record and not used.
**Consequences.** The catalogue has 80 playbooks (40 daily). No migration. Paper only; nothing is enabled by the build. On a premium of about 1.3 USD the trail starts at about 20 cents and a give-back of a fifth of the best is a few cents: one tick of a Gold option's mark can be the whole band, and a trailing target has no least give-back. An API test now drives the engine at that size (a level of 0.20 USD, a close at 0.30 after a best of 0.40). The fees of a round trip can be up to about 8 % of the premium under the exchange's cap, against a first exit level of 12 % (15 % less a fifth). Traceability HC-TR-262; GAPS #168.

## ADR-134 · The Friday weekly strangle held for its first day only, on BTC and ETH, as paper strategies (2026-11-24)
**Context.** The strategy lab's one-day table (`results-weekly-one-day-2026-11-24.txt`, `study_oneday.py`, bdaf809 on `research/exits`: 51 Friday expiries, 5 Dec 2025 to 20 Nov 2026, hourly marks, real option fees on the entry and on the exit; 36 sets of three stops, 108 rows) sells the Friday weekly strangle and closes it a fixed number of hours later. Sold on the Friday evening (164 hours before the settlement) and closed 20 hours later: BTC 3 % away +15.63 a week (88 % of weeks, total +797, worst -63.0, worst three -139.6, drawdown -63.0, halves +454 / +343), identical with the stop at triple and with no stop, +14.35 with the stop at double; ETH 4 % away +6.77 with the stop at triple (80 %, total +345, worst -18.3, drawdown -20.1, halves +222 / +123), +6.78 with no stop. The neighbouring rows, all with the stop at triple: the same 20-hour hold started on Saturday lost (BTC -5.65, ETH -1.82), on Sunday lost (BTC -2.99; -4.25 with no stop), on Monday earned about half (BTC +8.05, ETH +3.22). On BTC, sold two hours before the entry window it earned +17.21, two hours after it +8.92, four hours after +3.30, eight hours after -1.30; 2 % away in the window +17.06. By length of hold from the Friday evening, BTC: 4 hours +7.89, 8 hours +12.09, 12 hours +14.30, 16 hours +14.29 (worst week -81.7), 24 hours +13.33 (worst -103.6); ETH: 8 hours +6.05, 12 hours +7.17, 24 hours +5.31. So the result hangs on the price in one hour of one evening, and most of it is made in the first hours. One of the 36 sets is on Gold (15 weeks). Holding the same trades to settlement with the stop at triple earns +40.65 a week on BTC (worst -168.3) and +14.43 on ETH (the existing cards). The owner approved it with the lab ("Approve the Friday one-day weekly strangle on BTC and ETH as a paper strategy") and confirmed it in the build thread.
**Decision.** **Two playbooks** (`ONE_DAY_WEEKLY_PLAYBOOKS`): `weekly-fri-strangle-one-day` (BTC, 3 % away) and `eth-weekly-fri-strangle-one-day` (ETH, 4 % away), each the Friday window, expiry and strikes of that coin's weekly strangle with the stop at triple, plus `exitHourUtc: 12`. No new rule and no engine change: the timed exit of ADR-121 arms a `time` rule at the next occurrence of that hour, which after a Friday 16:00 to 17:00 UTC entry is Saturday 12:00 UTC (17:30 IST), 19 to 20 hours on. One wording change in the web: an exit hour not after the entry window's first hour is the next day's, and the Autopilot card now says "the next day" for it (the daily "out by 1:30 AM" cards read as before). The cards carry the cautions: one of the best rows of 108, with its neighbours falling away fast (the Saturday and Sunday starts lost, Monday's earned about half, and on BTC the edge is gone within hours of the entry window); half of the BTC gain in the first four hours and nearly all of the ETH gain in the first eight, so either a weekend effect or a Friday evening mark that cannot be sold at; and fills at the mark in the study and on paper alike. The row of a running trade on the Autopilot screen now reads an armed timed exit ("Closes at ..., whatever the profit") in place of "held to settlement", which also corrects the two daily out-early strategies of ADR-121.
**Rejected.** A hold of 4 hours (half the income for a quarter of the worst week): not what the owner approved. Building the exit as "N hours after the entry": the engine's timed exit is by the clock, and for a one-hour entry window the clock hour is the same thing to within the hour.
**Consequences.** The catalogue has 82 playbooks (21 weekly). No migration. Paper only; nothing is enabled by the build; the first evening they can enter is a Friday. An entry late in the window is closed after 19 hours, not 20. The paper record will show whether the pattern continues, not whether the Friday evening price is obtainable on the exchange: that needs the bid and ask, which the lab has asked the owner's leave to read. Traceability HC-TR-263; GAPS #169.

## ADR-135 · Every event of a trade is shown with its date and time, in one format (2026-11-24)
**Context.** The owner opened the Details of a closed Autopilot trade and could not tell when anything had happened: the exit rules read "fired 23 Nov 2026" with no time, the legs showed an entry and an exit price with no moment for either, and a switched-off rule said nothing about when ("everywhere we need timestamp otherwise we will not [know] when is it closed, when is it hit stop loss, buy back or whatever it is"). The screens had grown five ways of printing a moment: `fmtDateTime` ("23 Nov 2026, 21:32 IST") on the cards' started and closed, a date alone on a fired rule, a day and month on the Journal, a time with no year or zone in the Protect dialog and on an adjustment, and a time with no date on a triggered alert. Every one of these moments is already stored and sent by the API (`openedAt` and `closedAt` on a leg, `firedAt`, `createdAt` and `updatedAt` on a rule, `at` on an adjustment, `triggeredAt` on an alert).
**Decision.** One format for a moment in a trade's life, `fmtDateTime` (day, month, year, hour and minute on the reader's clock, with the zone), and it is shown wherever such a moment is named. Details: each leg that has filled says when it was opened and, once closed, when and why; a fired rule says when it fired and a switched-off rule when it was switched off (its `updatedAt`, which the engine and the routes set at that moment), beside its state tag and in ordinary case; a rule's hint says when it was last saved; an adjustment uses the same format; the Days tile stays day-level, since the Started row beside it has the time. The card's fired tag carries the time of the latest fired rule. The Journal gives opened and closed to the minute, and its CSV exports the two instants in full (it exported the UTC day). The Builder's saved list says when a closed trade closed. The Protect dialog's timed exit and an alert's "fired" use the format too. Two local helpers (`fmtWhen`, `fmtInstant`) and the alert's own time-only format are removed; the Journal's day helpers stay for its date badge and the equity curve. Three moments are named for what they are: a live leg's stored `openedAt` is when its order was sent (a resting order fills later and the fill time is not stored), so it reads "order sent"; a leg not yet filled shows no moment; a close booked by Reconcile reads "booked". Display only: nothing is stored or computed differently.
**Rejected.** A shorter format without the year or the zone on tight rows: two formats is how the screens drifted apart, and a trade read months later, or by someone in another zone, needs both. UTC everywhere: the trader's own clock is what the rest of the app shows, and the zone is printed.
**Consequences.** Rows are longer; on a leg the times go on their own line, after the P&L. A leg closed by settlement is stamped when the settlement job ran, and a resting exit when its fill was read, not at the event itself. A rule's "last saved" moves with every save of the Protect dialog, which rewrites the set, and the same save drops switched-off rules and their times. Pixel baselines of the Desk cards, Details, the Journal and the alerts move and need the CI refresh. Day-level dates stay as they are: option expiries, billing and admin dates, backtest days. The paper export the strategy lab reads already carried every one of these moments. Traceability HC-TR-264; GAPS #170.

## ADR-136 · The cautious daily strangles on BTC and ETH, as paper strategies (2026-11-24)
**Context.** After two spikes in a day (about 3,000 up on 23 Nov, about 4,000 down that night) stopped a leg on most of the evening strangles, the owner asked the strategy lab for "the best trade setup so that we are not loosing the money" and gave it leave to choose paper strategies; asked in the build thread, they said what they want: "some cautious trades by combining most of the features we have without loosing much capital and not to take huge profits ... then everyone will get confidence and then slowly we can increase and take risks", and "go, and test wings". The lab's stack table (`results-stack-2026-11-24.txt`, `study_stack.py`, 88d86d3 on `research/exits`): 108 daily expiries (8 Aug to 23 Nov 2026, the two spike-day expiries of 22 and 23 Nov among them), entered 20 hours before the settlement, 5-minute marks, real fees; 27 exit combinations (the stop at double or triple, a buy-back, a trail on the whole trade, a lock, a close at 90 %, three filters that skip a day) at 1, 1.5 and 2 % away on each coin, 162 rows. BTC 1.5 % away, mean premium 24.8: stop at double, buy-back at 5, trail from 25 % giving back 30 %: +5.30 a day (81 % of days, total +572, worst -21.6, worst three -56.5, drawdown -21.6, 13 days losing over a quarter of the premium, halves +290 / +283); the stop at double alone +6.69 (59 %, worst -25.9, drawdown -56.7); the stop at triple alone +8.94 (68 %, worst -91.2, drawdown -147.6). The 1 % strangle with the stop at triple, which most cards use: +12.60 (66 %, worst -105.8, worst three -254.2, drawdown -185.0). ETH 2 % away, mean premium 11.1: stop at double, buy-back at 0.25, trail from 50 % giving back 20 %: +2.59 (65 %, total +280, worst -21.9, worst three -42.7, drawdown -28.0, 22 such days, halves +132 / +148); the stop at triple alone +3.12 (drawdown -89.1); at 1 % with the stop at triple +4.91 (62 %, worst -49.6, drawdown -119.9).
**Decision.** **Two playbooks** (`CAUTIOUS_DAILY_PLAYBOOKS`), beside the existing ones: `evening-strangle-cautious` (BTC, 1.5 % away, `legStop: 2`, `legBuyBackAt: 5`, `takeProfitOfCredit: 0.25`, `takeTrailPct: 30`) and `eth-evening-strangle-cautious` (ETH, 2 % away, `legStop: 2`, `legBuyBackAt: 0.25`, `takeProfitOfCredit: 0.5`, `takeTrailPct: 20`). No new rule and no engine change: the leg stop (ADR-112), the buy-back at a price (ADR-123) and the trailing take-profit (ADR-103, ADR-113) are armed together from the playbook's own fields, as the API test shows by entering the BTC one through the job. They are built to lose little, not to earn much, and the cards say so first: less than half the income of the 1 % strangle, and still a losing day about one in five on BTC and one in three on ETH. They are a balance, not the row with the smallest losses: on BTC at 1.5 % the trail from 15 % with the stop at double lost less (worst -20.1, drawdown -20.4, 6 bad days) and earned +3.83; on ETH at 2 % the trail from 25 % with the buy-back had a drawdown of -21.9 and earned +1.82. **How the exits meet.** On BTC the buy-back made no measurable difference (+5.35 without it, the same worst day): the trail starts long before an option is down to 5 dollars; on ETH it helped a little (+2.29 without it). Once an option has been stopped at double, the trail cannot start: the loss taken is one option's premium and the other can earn back at most its own, short of a quarter (BTC) or half (ETH) of the whole. That option is then held to settlement on its own stop and buy-back, and a second stop makes the day about minus the whole premium. Once the trail is running, a stop cannot be reached before the trail's own exit except on a jump. After a buy-back the trail starts at once and closes the other option at about 1.4 times its price (the API test drives this).
**What it is not.** A capped loss. A stop limits a loss only as far as its fill: the study judges it on 5-minute closes, the engine on marks every two seconds, and in a spike a real exchange fills worse than either. Only bought wings cap a loss; the owner has asked the lab to test the same two with wings, and nothing is built from that until they see it.
**Rejected.** The stop at triple at the same distances (more income, a worst day four times as large on BTC): not what the owner asked for. Changing the existing strangles: existing strategies are not changed.
**Consequences.** The catalogue has 84 playbooks (42 daily). No migration. Paper only; nothing is enabled by the build. The two were chosen from 162 rows after seeing them, on one period, so the true result is expected to be worse than the table. The table is after real fees and the paper P&L is before them: under the exchange's fee cap a fill costs about 0.5 USD an option on the BTC trade (my arithmetic from the rule, not a measured figure), so 1 to 2 USD a day, a fifth to two fifths of the +5.30, and the paper record will read better than the card and has to be read after fees. A trailing target that closes a trade already carrying a stopped leg is booked with the reason `target` even at a net loss (the correction to `stopped` is made for a buy-back only; older than this change). The night of 23 to 24 Nov is not in the table. Before either is traded live the owner and the lab should agree what the paper record has to show. Traceability HC-TR-265; GAPS #171.

## ADR-137 · A card's backtest figures are quoted with real fill costs where the lab has them, starting with the cautious pair (2026-11-24)
**Context.** The strategy lab fitted the exchange's fee rule to the owner's own orders and measured how those orders filled (0af4cb9 on `research/exits`; the order file itself is outside the repo and holds personal figures). Fees: the rule the app carries (options 0.01 % of notional capped at 3.5 % of the premium, plus 18 % GST; ADR-063, GAPS #129) fitted 1,152 real orders with none misfit, market, limit and settlement among them. Fills: 946 real market orders, about nine in ten on BTC, filled worse than the mark by about 1 % on an option priced over 300 USD, about 2.5 % at 50 to 300 and 5 to 8 % under 50. Every backtest so far fills at the mark, so every card's figures are too good by about that much a fill. With that cost applied to every market fill (`results-stack-slippage-2026-11-24.txt`), the cautious pair of ADR-136 reads: BTC +4.12 a day (78 % of days, total +445, worst -22.9, worst three -63.8, drawdown -42.2, 16 days losing over a quarter of the premium, halves +210 / +235) where the mark-filled row was +5.30 (81 %, drawdown -21.6); ETH +2.28 (61 %, total +247, worst -22.7, drawdown -29.3, 29 such days, halves +113 / +134) where it was +2.59 (65 %, drawdown -28.0). The 1 % strangle with the stop at triple, with the same cost: BTC +11.63 (worst -108.3, drawdown -189.7), ETH +4.47 (worst -50.8, drawdown -123.4).
**Decision.** The two cautious cards quote the figures with fill costs as their own (`figures`, the claim, the backtest row and every comparison in the rationale), say that they are with fill costs, and give the mark-filled figures beside them so they can be read against the other cards. The source cited is the with-cost table and the mark-filled one. The cards of ADR-108 to ADR-134 are not re-quoted here: the lab has with-cost tables for the stack rows only, and a card is changed when its own row exists with costs.
**Consequences.** The cautious cards now read worse than their neighbours on a different basis; each says so. The BTC drawdown doubles with fill costs (-42.2 against -21.6), which the card states. The ETH cost is assumed from mostly BTC fills. No behaviour changes: the same two playbooks, the same rules. Catalogue unchanged at 84. Traceability HC-TR-265; GAPS #173 for the older cards, GAPS #129 for the fees.

## ADR-138 · An ETH daily strangle with wings and the trail alone, as a paper strategy (2026-11-24)
**Context.** The owner's direction is cautious strategies first: lose little, do not chase large profits, build confidence, then take more risk (ADR-136). The two cautious strangles of ADR-136 have no cap on a loss; the owner asked the lab to test wings on them ("go, and test wings"). The lab's wings table (`study_wings_stack.py`, `results-wings-stack-2026-11-24.txt` and, with fill costs, `results-wings-stack-slippage-2026-11-24.txt`; 0af4cb9 on `research/exits`; 38 rows, 108 daily expiries) says: on BTC the wings cost most of the income and no winged row is worth running; on ETH, sold 2 % away with wings 2 % of the spot further out (on average 3.9 % from the spot) and the trail alone, with fill costs, +2.30 a day, 90 % of days positive, total +242, worst day -34.0, worst three -71.5, drawdown -34.0, 5 days losing over a quarter of the net premium, halves +150 / +91, on 105 of the 108 days (2 without a marked wing, 1 without a net credit); premium collected 10.9, paid 3.2, net 7.7 on average; the largest possible loss 41.5 on average and 58.2 at most. Filled at the mark the same row reads +2.80, 95 %, worst -33.7. Beside it, with fill costs: no wings and the trail alone +2.95 (94 %, worst -77.6, no cap); the wings held to settlement +3.53 (worst -53.6); the cautious stack with wings +0.97 to +1.07. The owner said "go" in the build thread on 24 Nov 2026.
**Decision.** One new paper playbook beside the others, `eth-evening-strangle-wings-trail` (`WINGS_TRAIL_DAILY_PLAYBOOKS`): ETH, the evening window (16:00 to 17:00 UTC, 20 hours before the settlement), the call and put nearest 2 % from the spot sold, the options nearest 4 % from the spot bought, 100 lots; no stop, no buy-back; the whole trade trailed from half the net premium with a give-back of 20 % of its best, otherwise held to settlement. No engine change and no migration: `clockPlanFor` already takes the premium as the net of all four legs (`-proposal.entryCost`), so the trail's level is half the NET premium as in the study, and the trailing target closes every open leg, the wings with the rest. The card quotes the figures with fill costs (ADR-137), the mark-filled row beside them, the largest possible loss, and that it still loses. The card's exit words for a winged trade that has a take say "no stop" where they said "held to expiry".
**How it differs from the study.** The study buys the listed strike nearest 4 % that has a mark; Autopilot takes the listed strike nearest 4 % and skips the evening if that strike has no price, so it may skip a day the study traded. The study skips a day with no net credit; Autopilot skips when the net premium does not exceed the fees of its fills, which is stricter. The study judges the trail on 5-minute closes; the engine on its own ticks. Further: the study's wing is the nearest strike with a mark strictly beyond the sold one, so on a short ladder it trades a nearer wing, where Autopilot refuses a strike past the end of the ladder by more than one step or one that lands on the sold strike; the study's sold strikes are likewise the nearest with a mark; the study stops judging the trail five minutes before the settlement and settles on that bar's spot, the engine on the settlement price; the study sizes at 0.01 ETH a lot, Autopilot by the trader's own lot-size setting. The table is after fees and with fill costs; the paper book fills at the mark and its P&L is before fees, so the paper record will read better than even the mark-filled +2.80 (the reviewer's estimate, not computed from data: 0.6 to 0.9 USD a day of fees on eight fills) and has to be read after fees (GAPS #173). The card's words for the level say "net premium" for every playbook with bought options, which also corrects the older winged cards that take a profit (they said "the premium collected" for a level that was always on the net).
**Consequences.** Chosen from 38 rows after seeing them, on one period, and its distance (2 %) and trail (from 50 %, give-back 20 %) were themselves taken from the 162-row cautious table of ADR-136 on the same 108 days; the wings are far strikes priced by the exchange's model and nothing shows one can be bought at that price; the fill cost on ETH is assumed from mostly BTC fills; the second half of the period earned less than the first. Catalogue 85 (43 daily, 34 on ETH). Not switched on by the build. Traceability HC-TR-266; GAPS #174.

## ADR-139 · The Autopilot screen as a summary and four full-width views (2026-11-24)
**Context.** The owner reported that not all the data on the Autopilot screen is visible and asked for a more readable, professional screen, and to be able to see what was traded on a particular day and where it won and lost. With 85 playbooks the screen was one scroll of about 10,400 px before any trade (measured headless at 1536 px); the open trades (28 that day, 72 after the evening entries) were cards in a 360 px side column in no order; the results sat under all 85 strategies; the screen drew the newest 25 of the closed trades and 60 of the log lines with no way to more; row text was 11 px and a strategy name was cut off in the open-trade cards and the log. Two independent design reviews (a trading-workflow one and a readability one, run as subagents on the code and screenshots) agreed on these points.
**Decision.** The switch, Stop everything and a summary stay on top (open trades and how many need attention, open profit or loss, today, capital in use, every closed trade net of fees, strategies on); the introduction moves behind "How Autopilot works". Below, four views each take the full width (`AutopilotPage.tsx`, tabs `Open trades`, `Closed trades`, `Strategies`, `Log`), each with its count; the expiry and coin buttons and a new search by strategy name narrow all four. A view that is not shown stays mounted (the `hidden` attribute), so what was opened in it is kept. (1) Open trades are a table, worst first: no live price, close to a stop, losing, on track, and within each the largest loss first; "close to a stop" is the health reading's own (three quarters of a whole-trade stop used) or a sold option whose price is three quarters of the way from what it was sold at to its own stop (`NEAR_LEG_STOP`, `legNearStop`: most strangles are stopped option by option and have no whole-trade stop, so the reading alone would never flag them); the summary counts the first two groups as needing attention; the order is held while the pointer is on the table, so that a row does not move under a press on Exit now; health is a shape and a word as well as a colour; the levels in words and the loss used are behind a Levels button in the row; the table has no sideways scroll at any width (the owner's word: "for open trades scroll should not come"): its cells wrap, and columns give way to lines under the name as the screen narrows. (2) Closed trades (`AutopilotResultsPanel.tsx`) are grouped by the day they closed in the viewer's time zone, each day with its count, won and lost and net after fees; one day and the won or lost ones can be picked; rows are drawn 100 at a time with Show more; the tables by strategy and by days to expiry follow. The server sends its newest 500 closed trades (`AUTOPILOT_TRADES_SHOWN`, was 100); the screen says how many older ones are counted and not listed, with a selection on as well, the tab counts every closed trade of the selection, and the oldest day listed is marked as a part of that day when older trades were left out. (3) Strategies keep their rows and every word they had; the exit rule reads as short tags in a fixed order (`exitTags`: the stop first, then wings, take or trail, steps, floor, timed exit, buy-back) with the sentence kept under "What it does"; they can be narrowed to switched on, in a trade or switched off and sorted by paper result or backtest average. (4) The log can be narrowed to one kind of line and to one day and is drawn 100 lines at a time. Row text is 12 to 13 px and numbers are right-aligned. No engine change, no strategy change, no migration; Exit now, Open on the Desk and Stop everything are moved, not rewritten.
**Rejected.** Drawing only the view that is shown: the open rows of a strategy's details and the day picked would be lost on every switch, and the existing tests read all four at once. Charts or an equity curve: about 100 closed trades over 66 strategy-and-coin rows is noise. Replacing the strategy name by attribute columns (coin, distance, wings): the playbook names are the identity used in the log, the results and the research thread. A "next entries" figure in the summary: the weekly playbooks' entry days are not exposed to the screen, so the count could be wrong.
**Consequences.** "Lost" in the day groups is every closed trade that is not above zero after the estimated fees, a flat one included. A day is the viewer's calendar day of the close, not the settlement day: trades settled at 17:30 IST and one closed at 12:30 AM IST fall on the same date. The day totals and the log's day list cover only what the server sends (500 closed trades, 200 log lines); paging to older rows is open (GAPS #175). The strategies view is still long (about 7,900 px with all 85 shown) because a row lists each coin's record; the Show and search controls shorten it. The screen was looked at headless with an empty account at 1536 px and 390 px in the dark theme; it was not looked at with the owner's rows by me, and the light theme and the visual baselines were not run (GAPS #175). From the code review of this change (fixed before the commit): the attention count ignored option-by-option stops, the note on unlisted trades was missing under a selection, and the tie-break put every trade without a whole-trade stop last. Left open from it (GAPS #175): the order is held for the pointer only, not for keyboard focus or touch; the day groups are tested with days computed by the screen's own function, with no fixed zone and no midnight case.

## ADR-140 · The public home page is HapieCoin's own, with pictures of the product; plans are not on sale yet (2026-11-25)
**Context.** The home page and the sign-in side panel still carried the competitor's copy and section order ("Trade Crypto Options Like a Pro", a stats strip, a with / without table, feature tiles, "coming soon" exchanges), and promised a Free plan that ADR-107 switched off. The owner made a home page of our own the last step before deployment, asked for it to be built from our functionality and screenshots, asked that the pictures do not show how the Autopilot strategies work, said Autopilot is to trade live later and not only on paper, and decided that plans are not sold until the pricing research is done.
**Decision.** (1) **The page** (`apps/web/src/app/page.tsx`, copy in `content/landing.ts`) follows the order a trade happens: a hero with one promise ("Know your worst case before you place the trade") over a full-width picture of the Desk, the live market tiles, Build / Protect / Review, Autopilot, Safety with the one connected exchange, and Plans. Every claim names something built; nothing is counted, ranked or attributed to customers. (2) **The pictures** are the product itself (`apps/web/public/shots`, `ProductShot.tsx`): taken from the local app with a throwaway user, sample trades served to the browser only, live public prices; one file per theme, both in the page, CSS shows the one for the theme. Each is captioned "Sample trades · live market prices". The Autopilot picture shows health and profit or loss only: strategy names, strikes, exit levels and stops are taken out. The samples are illustrations and are never called results. (3) **Autopilot** is described as it is: "Paper today · live next". (4) **Plans are not on sale**: one switch, `PLANS_ON_SALE` in `content/landing.ts`, read from the environment (`NEXT_PUBLIC_PLANS_ON_SALE`, off unless it says `true`; amended the same day so that sales are turned on at deploy time and the end-to-end stack, which sets it, keeps the buying flow tested). When it is on the home page links to the plans again (`HomePage.tsx`). The home page says "coming soon" with the support address, the hero button is "Request early access" (sign-up), and `/plans` shows a visitor who is not an admin no prices, no plan cards and no way to pay, with how to ask for early access; what a trader already has (plan, limits, payments) stays. Access is granted by hand from the admin screen (ADR-107). (5) The header and footer links follow the page and work from every public page (`/#how`, `/#autopilot`, `/#safety`, `/#plans`); the sign-in panel carries the same promise.
**Rejected.** Drawn illustrations in place of screenshots (the 8 and 21 Nov mocks): the owner asked for the product. The owner's own trades in the pictures: they are personal, and the first mock was turned down for showing them. Showing only winning trades as HapieCoin's record: the paper record has losing days, so the pictures are samples and say so. Prices on the home page: they would live in two places, and they are not decided. Naming exchanges as "coming soon": none of them has an options API we have integrated. Removing the `/plans` route: a trader with a granted plan reads its dates and limits there.
**Consequences.** `FeatureTabs.tsx` and `TerminalIllustration.tsx` are gone with the copy they drew. The pictures are static files: they age as the screens change and are retaken by hand. On a phone a whole-screen picture is too small to read. The switch hides buying in the app; the checkout routes of the API are unchanged. Every new sign-up waits for a plan granted by hand until the switch is turned on. "Live next" is a public statement of intent: live Autopilot still needs its own ADR, a verified margin figure (GAPS #109) and an answer on the rules for algorithmic trading in India (ADR-108). Open items in GAPS #177.

## ADR-141 · The Autopilot strategy builder: saved strategies beside the built-in ones, given to plans, live only after approval from paper (2026-11-25)
**Context.** Every Autopilot strategy is a fixed entry in `packages/pricing/src/playbooks.ts`; a new one needs a code change. The owner asked for a builder: an admin creates, saves, edits and deletes strategies with every setting the current ones have, customers switch them on, and "both paper and live and whatever we approve from paper only those will go to live". The owner asked for research and mock screens first and for the current Autopilot to stay untouched. The design is `docs/design/autopilot-strategy-builder.md`, with `docs/research/autopilot-live-compliance.md` and `docs/research/delta-margin-verification.md`; the mocks are at https://claude.ai/artifact/Ni223PVQjgjhD7AK4LeVdQ. Nothing is built by this record.
**Decision.** (1) Saved strategies live in a table of their own and reach the engine through one loader beside the built-in list; the built-in strategies, their ids and their trades do not change. (2) A strategy moves Draft, Backtested, On paper, Approved for live; Live is per customer; Retired stops new entries. A change to a strategy that has traded saves a new version with an empty record and no approval. (3) Approval for live needs: backtest net positive after costs, at least 30 days on paper, at least 20 closed paper trades, paper net positive, the margin estimate checked, and the admin having read the trades. (4) A strategy whose loss is not capped by bought options never goes live. (5) The owner approves for live at first; the permission can later be given to other admins. (6) A strategy is given to plans; a user may switch it on when their plan is one of them. Plans stay hidden from users (ADR-140) and the admin gives each user a plan. (7) The backtest starts as figures from the research thread entered by hand; the backtest inside the app follows. (8) Live starts with the owner's account and a couple of users the owner selects. (9) Delta and a lawyer are not contacted now, on the owner's word that the product is not public and only known people use it for a few days.
**Rejected.** Editing the built-in strategies in place: their records and open trades would change meaning. Live in the first version: the order path assumes a person at the screen. Live for an uncapped strategy with stops: a stop is a market order and can fill far from its level. Waiting for the in-app backtest before the builder: it is the largest piece and would hold the builder back.
**Consequences.** Paper only (ADR-108) stands until the live step is built under its own decision record; this record approves the design, not a live order. The margin defect (GAPS #109) must be fixed and checked on a test trade before that. The two open questions (Delta's Terms clause 7.2 for a platform placing orders on a customer's key, and whether the service counts as advice or portfolio management) return before live reaches anyone outside the owner's selected group or the product goes public; the selected users carry the clause 7.2 risk on their own accounts and are to be told. Plans need a new link to strategies. Prices and benefits are undecided; a first proposal is section 8 of the design note.

## ADR-142 · Saved Autopilot strategies: one row per version, always a clock playbook, the audience on the row (2026-11-25)
**Context.** ADR-141 decided the builder; this is how its first step (saved strategies on paper, HC-TR-269..276) is built. The owner asked that trend strategies go in the same editor, with the same exits as the clock ones, and that the running Autopilot is not affected.
**Decision.** (1) **A table of its own**, `autopilot_saved_strategies` (migrations 0032 to 0034: 0032 creates it, 0033 renames its two indexes to carry the table's name by dropping and recreating them, 0034 adds the index that holds one version of a family on paper; no existing table is touched). One row is one version, and its id (`sv_...`) is what Autopilot trades it under, so a version's trades, log lines and results are its own with no extra bookkeeping. New settings on a version that has traded become the next version of the same family; publishing it retires the other and moves the switch of every trader who had the other on to the new one (the engine still checks that each may use it). (2) **Always a clock playbook.** A saved strategy becomes the `Playbook` the engine already runs, with a `clock` (the window may be the whole day) and, when set, the market conditions the engine already judges (`conditions`: the price against its average, the volatility rank, the weekdays). So "by the clock" and "by market conditions" are one model and one editor, and every exit of ADR-112 to ADR-124 is available to both. Nothing in `packages/pricing` changed. (3) **The engine reads them through one call** (`savedCatalogue`), once a pass, and adds the rows at stage `paper` to the built-in list; with none, it iterates the built-in list itself. A failure of that read is logged and the built-in playbooks trade on. (4) **The audience is on the row**: admins only, named users (`user_ids`), or plans (`plan_ids`), the way a plan holds its menu items. An admin may always use a strategy. (5) **Settings let go**: a trader's saved settings drop a saved strategy that is no longer published or no longer theirs, instead of refusing the whole save. (5a) **One conversion, one clock.** A saved strategy becomes a playbook in one place (`savedPlaybookOf` in `packages/schema`, written out structurally because that package does not depend on `packages/pricing`); the engine and the trader's screen both use it, and the compiler checks the shape against `Playbook` at each. The trader's Autopilot view carries the saved strategies they may use and the ones they have traded (`SavedCard`), so the screen needs no admin route; a strategy that only sits in a trader's settings or log and is not theirs is never sent, and publishing a new version moves the switch only of the traders who may use it. Times are written as the built-in strategies write them ("9:30 to 10:30 PM IST"). (5b) **A built-in strategy is copied in the editor, not on the server** (HC-TR-280): the admin page lists the built-in strategies read-only, and Copy as my own fills the editor from one (`savedFormFromBuiltIn`) so that what is saved is an ordinary draft through the ordinary create route. The copy trades under its own id beside the built-in one and its record starts empty; its backtest figures are not copied; its name is never cut (one too long is left for the admin to write). A built-in strategy the editor cannot hold whole is not offered as a copy and says why. (6) **Backtest figures are entered by hand** with a required note of where they come from, and are cleared when the settings change. (7) **What the settings refuse** (from the code review of 25 Nov 2026): any exchange but Delta Exchange India; a window longer than six hours unless it enters once per expiry (the engine's "once per window" is six hours, so a longer window would enter again after each close); a premium or a buy-back price with more than one coin (they are money of one coin); an expiry nearer than 0.04 days; a trail of the rest without the stop that starts it; a profit take at the whole premium; a user or a plan that does not exist. (8) **Writes that cannot miss a trade**: the edit in place and the delete carry "no trade was ever opened from it" in the write itself; a strategy on paper is retired, never deleted; an entry checks inside its own transaction that the strategy is still published and holds the row until the trade is written; a published row whose settings no longer pass the schema is left out of the pass and logged.
**Rejected.** A second playbook shape for market conditions with Autopilot's 10 % profit exit: two editors and two sets of exits for the owner to learn. Moving the built-in strategies into the table: it rewrites the ids open trades and records depend on. A version column on the strategies table instead of an id per version: every query of results would need it. A link table for plans and users: more joins for lists that hold a handful of ids.
**Consequences.** A saved strategy must collect premium: the engine's plan for a clock playbook refuses an entry whose premium does not exceed the fees, so a bought structure (a debit spread on a trend) cannot be saved yet (GAPS #178). Weekdays and hours are UTC in the settings, as the engine keeps them; the screens and the entry sentence (`savedEntryText`) show India time and India days, converting through one rule (`savedIstWeekday`: a window from 19:00 UTC opens on the next India day). The admin screens (PR B) and the trader's list (PR C) follow; until PR C a published strategy can be switched on only through the API. Approval for live, the mandate and the in-app backtest are later steps of ADR-141; paper only stands.

## ADR-143 · A saved Autopilot strategy may be a bought structure, with exits on the whole trade (2026-11-25)
**Context.** A saved strategy had to collect premium (ADR-142): its plan was the clock playbook's, which refuses an entry unless the premium collected exceeds the fees. So the six built-in strategies entered on a market condition (bought call and put spreads, a butterfly, a sold put spread, an iron condor, a covered call) could be neither built nor copied in the editor. The owner asked that an admin be able to create strategies like them. Their exits are of another kind than the clock ones: a share of the most the trade can make or lose, and so many days before expiry.
**Decision.** (1) **Legs**: a leg by listed strikes may sit at the money (0 strikes) or in the money (`itm`), and may be a multiple of the strategy's lots (`ratio`, 2 to 5). (2) **Three exits on the whole trade**: `targetOfMax` (a share of the most the trade can make at expiry, which `takeTrailPct` may turn into a trail), `stopOfMax` (a share of the most it can lose) and `timeStopDte` (days before expiry). They ride on the playbook's `exits`, where the built-in trend playbooks keep theirs; no built-in clock playbook uses that field, and nothing in `packages/pricing` changed. (3) **Rules in the settings**: a strategy sells at least one option; a target on the most a trade can make needs that most to be limited (no more bought than sold, of each kind, lot for lot), so a lone bought option or a bought straddle, which the engine could never give a plan, is refused where it is typed; a stop on the most it can lose needs every sold option covered by a bought one; one profit level, not two; the exit before expiry is at least one day and leaves the trade at least a day to live. (4) **A plan of its own** (`savedPlanFor`), taken by the engine for a saved id only: without whole-trade exits it is the clock plan, to the figure; with a target on the most it can make, the level is that share plus the fees of both fills, so that reaching it is that profit after fees (as the built-in trend playbooks have it), and a level not below the most the trade can make is no trade; without such a target the structure must collect more premium than the fees; the profit levels on the premium collected (a take, a lock, steps) need a premium collected; a level that is not a number or rounds to nothing is no plan. Each refusal has its own sentence in the log (`savedPlanRefusal`). The two plans of the built-in playbooks are not touched. (5) **A futures leg stays out**: Autopilot has no margin figure for one, built-in or saved.
**Rejected.** A second kind of saved strategy with the trader's choice of trail or target, as the built-in trend playbooks have: two editors and two sets of exits. Letting a bought structure run with no target: nothing would take its profit before expiry. Checking at save time whether a structure pays or collects premium: it depends on the prices of the day, so the check is at entry.
**Consequences.** A copy of a built-in trend strategy is close, not the same: its exit is fixed by the admin (the built-in one lets each trader choose trail or target), with a whole-day window it enters once per expiry (the built-in one may enter again after a cool-down), and it trades under its own id. The clock exits on a bought structure (a stop on its sold leg, a buy-back) are allowed where a sold option exists and have never been backtested: the rule that a strategy is published only with backtest figures is what stands between them and a trader. The editor and the copy of the trend strategies followed on 26 Nov 2026 (HC-TR-284, HC-TR-285): the editor's legs by strikes out of, at or in the money and of 1 to 5 times the lots, three presets, the three exits in per cent and days, the trader's card wording them; five of the six built-in trend strategies copy (not the covered call), as a strategy that may enter at any time of day, once per expiry, with no furthest expiry short of the settings' limit of 400 days; a daily strategy entered a day out or more reads "on the first expiry at least N days out". A copy of a trend strategy is not the trade Autopilot makes of the built-in one, and the editor says each difference: Autopilot takes the built-in one's profit at 10 % of the most it can make (`AUTOPILOT_TARGET_PCT`, closing there or trailing from there by the trader's choice) whatever target the playbook names, and the copy starts with the named target (50 % or 75 %), which the admin may set to 10; the built-in one may enter again on the same expiry and trades on every venue the API serves; it has no backtest figures to copy. The presets that pay premium fill in a target, because one saved without it is refused at every entry. Paper only.

## ADR-144 · The backtest of a saved Autopilot strategy runs in the app, on the exchange's fine-grained candles (2026-11-26)
**Context.** Step 2 of the builder (ADR-141). A saved strategy's backtest figures are typed in by hand from the research thread's scripts. The app's own backtest (`runBacktest`, ADR-077) steps once a day and knows a target, a stop and a time exit only: it cannot judge a stop on one sold option, a trail, steps, a lock, a timed exit or a buy-back, which happen inside the day. The app's own fine prices (`instrument_marks`, 5 minutes) are kept for 7 days. Delta Exchange India serves `MARK:` candles of every option it listed, expired ones included: checked on 26 Nov 2026 for a BTC call that expired on 10 Nov 2025 and one that expired on 10 Aug 2026, 433 five-minute bars each over 36 hours.
**Decision.** (1) **Prices**: the exchange's public mark candles, downloaded by the API when a backtest runs and kept in a table; 5 minutes for a daily strategy, one hour for weekly, monthly and longer ones, as the research scripts used. No keys are involved. (2) **One trade** is replayed by a pure function, `simulateTrade` (apps/api/src/clock-sim.ts): the rules `enter()` writes from the plan, judged as `evaluateRules` judges them and with the helpers both use (`RULE_KIND_ORDER`, `trailFloorUsd`, `restTrailFloorUsd`, `stepLots`, `openProfitPct`, `nextHourUtc`), the plan itself coming from `clockPlanFor` / `savedPlanFor`; every close is booked in whole cents, as the paper book books it, and a test walks three trades Autopilot entered through the rules engine, the settler and the replay and compares what each booked. It sits in the API, not in `packages/pricing`, because it needs those helpers from the schema package and the plan from the engine, and pricing depends on neither. (3) **Fees**: the venue's own rule (`fillFee`, `settlementFee`) on the entry fills, every exit fill and a settlement in the money. (4) **What a replay is not**, said with every figure made from it: fills are at the bar's mark (no bid / ask, no slippage); a bar is one price where the live engine looks every two seconds, so a level crossed and uncrossed inside a bar is not seen and an exit is booked at the bar's price; a bar in which an open leg has no mark is skipped and counted. (5) **Checked against research** before the button is offered: the replay must reproduce the typed figures of a few built-in strategies within a stated margin. (6) **Hand entry stays**: a setting the app cannot replay (the volatility-rank condition, Gold before the app recorded it) says so and the figures are typed as before. (7) The engine, the rules engine and the built-in list do what they did: the one edit is the word `export` on a constant of the engine file.
**Rejected.** Extending the daily `runBacktest`: a once-a-day look would give figures for a stop or a buy-back that look grounded and are not. Using only the app's own recorded marks: seven days. Moving the rule arithmetic out of the rules engine into a shared module first: it would touch the code that manages the open paper trades, for no change in what it does; the drift this leaves is GAPS #180.
(8) **The run** (HC-TR-287, HC-TR-288): for every day of the period it asks what the engine asks at the entry window, through the engine's own `proposalFor` and `savedPlanFor`, holds one trade at a time and one per expiry where the strategy says so, prices the legs at the entry instant from their candles (a leg chosen by premium among the options priced then, from the money outwards; every leg is priced before a side is left out after a move, as the template is materialised whole) and replays the trade. An entry is tried on every bar of the window's first hour and then once an hour, so a trade that ends inside a long window frees the next entry as it does for the engine, to the hour; `CLOCK_ONCE_MS` is exported from the engine file for that, its only change. The strike ladder of an entry is the one recorded at the last settlement before it (`chain_eod`), since no finer record of what was listed exists; where the price has moved towards or past an end of that recording, the strikes beyond it that the exchange was pricing at the entry instant are added (a candidate on the grid the nightly chain history walks, ADR-098, counts only when one of the options asked about has a mark then; at most 25 a side; for a leg counted in strikes the look goes on until that many strikes and two more are known each side of the price, for a leg by per cent until two steps past it, and for a leg by premium only when its walk reaches the end of the known strikes). 120 days back on 5-minute prices for a strategy on the next expiries, a year on hourly ones for the others, never before the recorded history. Candles are kept in `mark_candles` (one row per series and resolution, nothing past the moment of the download); runs in `autopilot_saved_backtests` (migration 0035), worked in the background of the API process, one at a time per strategy, read as failed when their process is gone. (9) **Figures of a run** are put on the strategy by the server, with the run's id, period, prices and limits beside the numbers; the form's schema has no such field, so typed figures can never claim to be computed, and figures sent back unchanged by a form keep the mark.
**Consequences.** Built in four parts: the replay of one trade (HC-TR-286), the prices and the run (HC-TR-287, HC-TR-288), the check against the research figures, the screen. The check was run on 26 Nov 2026 (docs/research/backtest-in-app-check.md): three built-in BTC strategies over the research's own periods, the average within 13 % (13 % under, 3 % under, 5 % over), the share of winning trades within a point, the trades the same (106, 106, 51); four headline figures compared, not the trades one by one, and no margin was set beforehand, so whether that is close enough is the owner's call. Its first round found the days the price had run past the recorded strikes dropped, which flattered the figures; the ladder is extended for that, a rule made on the same days it is measured on. The screen (part 4, HC-TR-289, HC-TR-290; 26 Nov 2026): a Backtest action on each row of /admin/autopilot-strategies opens a dialog of its own, not a part of the editor, because a run tests the settings as saved and the editor holds unsaved ones. It shows the run's progress (asked every two seconds; it goes on when the dialog is closed), then the four figures on one line with the period and the price granularity on the line above, the totals before and after fees, the limits open by default, the days with no entry by reason and the trades. Use these figures is offered only for a finished run with trades on the settings the strategy still has. The list and the editor say whether figures are a backtest in the app or entered by hand; figures changed by hand in the editor become hand-entered (the form never holds the mark of a run), and the editor refuses to save them until the source sentence is changed too, so a backtest's own sentence never stands behind figures it did not produce; new settings saved over them clear them. The period of a run is shown only once it is done (the server writes it at the end), and the runs are read afresh each time the dialog opens, so a run made stale by an edit is never offered from an old answer. Paper only.

## ADR-145 · Approval of a saved Autopilot strategy for live is a record on a strategy that stays on paper (2026-11-26)
**Context.** Step 3 of the builder (ADR-141; design doc 3.4 and the owner's decisions of 25 Nov 2026): a strategy goes live only after it has been approved from its paper run. The owner kept the numbers: at least 30 days on paper, at least 20 closed paper trades, backtest and paper result both positive after fees, and a strategy whose loss is not limited never goes live. Approval is its own permission that starts with one named admin. Live itself is step 4, with its own decision record, the margin fix (GAPS #109) and deployment first.
**Decision.** (1) **Not a stage.** The row stays at stage `paper` and gains an `approval` (who, when, and the paper record it was given on). Every place that trades or lists a saved strategy tests for `paper`, the engine among them, and none of them reads the approval: an approved strategy trades on paper exactly as before. A fifth stage was rejected for that reason. (2) **The paper record of a version** is read from the Autopilot log and the trades opened under the version's id, in ONE account: the one that took the most entries of it (the first to enter, then the lowest id, when two took as many). Accounts trade their own lot sizes and can miss entries or enter on different passes, so adding accounts together, or letting a different account stand for each entry, mixes sizes and counts an entry twice (the first build did that; the code review of 26 Nov 2026 showed a record could pass on it). One account's trades are one consistent run. Closed and net are counted as the trader's own results count them (the P&L at the closing marks less the fees estimated at entry), in whole cents. The record says when it was published, its first and latest paper trade, how many accounts traded it, and that the log behind it keeps 90 days; its skip lines are that account's log lines by reason, which the engine writes when the reason changes, not once per entry skipped. (3) **The checks**: backtest average positive, 30 days since the first paper trade (not since the publish: a strategy published and left switched off has not been on paper), 20 closed trades, paper net positive after the estimated fees, loss limited by bought options (judged from its settings). All five block. The sixth of the design, the margin estimate checked against the exchange, is shown as open and does not block: the app cannot make it today, and live waits on it anyway. The seventh, that the approver has read the trades and the skipped entries, is a confirmation sent with the request. The permission and the checks are read again inside the write, on the locked row, and the audit line is written in the same transaction. An account with the authenticator on confirms both steps with its code (ADR-086). (4) **Who**: a field on the user, `can_approve_live`, off for everyone and given by name in the database; the admin role alone reads the record and cannot approve or withdraw, and the field without the admin role or on a deactivated account is nothing. (5) **It is kept with what it was given on**: the paper record and the backtest figures of that moment. **It ends** when it is withdrawn (at once, the strategy stays on paper), when the backtest figures change, when the strategy is retired or published again, and it never carries to a new version. It does not end by itself when the record later stops passing: the screen shows the checks as they are now beside the approval. (6) **Traders see nothing of it** until live exists: the word on a card would say live can be had.
**Consequences.** Migration 0036 (two additive columns). Routes `GET .../{id}/approval`, `POST .../{id}/approve`, `POST .../{id}/withdraw-approval` under `/v1/admin/autopilot-strategies` (HC-TR-291, HC-TR-292, HC-TR-293); the admin screen (HC-TR-294): an Approval action on a row that is on paper opens a dialog of its own with the checks first (pass, does not pass, open, each with its sentence), then the paper record under a heading that says it is one account of however many traded it, the closed trades and the skip lines open for reading, a box the approver ticks, and Approve for live; an approved strategy shows who approved it, when, on which record and backtest, and beside that what the checks say today, with Withdraw approval behind one more question. The list marks an approved row beside its stage, which stays On paper. An admin without the permission reads everything and is told why they cannot approve. The checks themselves are written once, in the schema package (`savedApprovalChecks`), for the server and the screens. Nothing places, enables or switches an order: Autopilot stays paper only. Step 4 has to decide what an approval withdrawn, a version retired, or a record that no longer passes does to live trades that are open, whether a wider audience needs the approval looked at again, and has to close the margin check before the first live order. The API is deployed before the web: the saved strategy now carries a required `approval` field.

## ADR-146 · The margin percent of a position grows per USD above the venue's threshold, not per contract; supersedes the unit clause of ADR-091 and ADR-095 (2026-11-26)
**Context.** The margin estimate of a sold option (ADR-091) and of a futures leg (ADR-095) added `initial_margin_scaling_factor × contracts` percent to the listed percent, from the first contract. GAPS #109 held the unit as unverified. The research of 25 Nov 2026 (docs/research/delta-margin-verification.md) read Delta's margin explainer, its API field descriptions and its public product list: the percent is flat up to a position size in USD (`max_leverage_notional`, 200,000 USD on a production BTC option, 100,000 on the testnet) and grows by the factor for every USD above it. The code never read that field. Below the threshold the old figure was at most 1.4 % high; above it it was low (about 26 % at 5,000 lots, 59 % at 10,000). Step 4 of the Autopilot strategy builder (live) needs the margin figure right before the first order it places by itself.
**Decision.** `IM% = initial_margin + initial_margin_scaling_factor × max(0, position USD − max_leverage_notional)`, with the position measured as contracts × contract value × spot for an option and × price for a future, each leg its own position. `max_leverage_notional` is carried from both product reads (the trading client's `getProduct` and the public list) to `shortOptionMarginUsd`, `futuresMarginUsd`, the live preview and the estimate stored at entry (ADR-128). A product that lists a factor above zero and no threshold gets no figure (null): the old arithmetic is not fallen back on. The live preview refuses a placement that holds such a leg and names the cause, alone or beside other legs: a sold option with no figure otherwise passed with no margin check at all, the rule written for a venue that lists no margin data (found in the code review). A threshold that is empty, not a number or below zero counts as absent, and so does a factor below zero; the public list leaves a threshold that is not a decimal out instead of failing the whole list. The rest of ADR-091 and ADR-095 stands: the option's mark is added, no spread relief, buys before sells, refusal before the first order.
**Built from documents, not from a position.** The formula is Delta's documented one and the magnitudes at the listed limits rule out the other readings, but no figure has been compared with the margin of a real position. Still open in GAPS #109: the owner's testnet check (the predicted 1,028.34 at 2,000 lots is a test in the code), whether the trader's chosen leverage sets the margin of a sold option (the figure is at the highest leverage and is a floor if it does), whether a sold vanilla option's margin includes its premium, the account's margin mode (portfolio margin is another model), and how a position's size is measured when an order adds to an open one (the estimate sizes the order alone).
**Consequences.** HC-TR-295. Estimates of the sizes traded so far move by cents (a 100-lot strangle: 143.02 to 143.00). Above the threshold the preview now asks for more and refuses sooner. Stored estimates of open and closed trades are not recomputed. No migration. Live Autopilot must not size on this figure until the testnet check is recorded.

## ADR-147 · Live Autopilot is per trader, per strategy version and per exchange account, by a signed mandate; the mandate is built first, as a record nothing reads (2026-11-26)
**Context.** Step 4 of the Autopilot strategy builder (ADR-141) is the first time Autopilot may place a real order. The live order path of today assumes a person at every entry: the typed LIVE word, the Mindful pause, the prices seen at a preview. An unattended entry has none of them. The design (docs/design/autopilot-strategy-builder.md 3.5) replaces them by a mandate signed once. The owner agreed on 26 Nov 2026 to build step 4 in four parts with live switched off: the margin formula (ADR-146), the mandate, the engine's live entry behind a server switch, the screens; and that switching live on is a separate step that waits for deployment on fixed IPs, the owner's testnet margin check (GAPS #109) and the owner's word.
**Decision.** A mandate (table `autopilot_mandates`, migration 0037) belongs to one trader, one saved strategy row (a version: new settings are a new row, so they need a new mandate) and one of the trader's own exchange accounts. It holds the lots per trade (at most the strategy's), the most capital it may use, a loss limit for the day and for the week, the number of trades open at once (whole US dollars as typed), and the four statements in the words signed. Signing needs the typed LIVE word, an account with the authenticator ON and its current code (an account without one cannot sign), and live trading as part of the trader's plan (whether the plan includes it, not the month's count: signing uses nothing, and the count is the engine's to read at each entry). It is refused unless the strategy may trade live: on paper with a standing approval (ADR-145), its loss limited by bought options (the owner's decision 4), no exit the rules engine holds on a live trade (profit steps, the buy-back of a cheap option), the trader in its audience, the account the trader's own and on the strategy's venue, no more trades open at once than the strategy has coins, and no other mandate of that trader for it that has not ended. Settings that cannot be read (a row written past the schema) are a refusal in words. Pause and end are never held behind a code or a check: stopping is always allowed. Resume asks for the code (and refuses an account whose authenticator was switched off since), and checks again that the strategy may trade live and is still the trader's to use. An ended mandate is final. Disconnecting an exchange account ends its mandates in the same transaction; one whose account went another way reads ended, takes no step but End, and does not hold up a new signing. Every step is audited inside its transaction.
**What a mandate does not do.** Nothing reads it yet. The engine still writes paper trades only and the manual live routes still refuse an Autopilot trade. The limits become hard stops on NEW entries when the engine's live entry is built (its own plan and decision); open trades keep their exits. A mandate whose strategy lost its approval, or was retired, stays as it is and reads `standing: false`, as does one whose trader was taken out of the strategy's audience: no new entry would be taken on it.
**Rejected.** Reusing the manual place route with a stored confirmation: that route checks the prices a person saw at a preview, and an unattended entry has none. One mandate per trader for every strategy: the limits and the account are a decision per strategy.
**Consequences.** Routes `GET /v1/autopilot/mandates`, `POST /v1/autopilot/mandates`, `POST /v1/autopilot/mandates/{id}/pause|resume|end` (HC-TR-296, HC-TR-297, HC-TR-298). No screen yet (it follows with the engine). GAPS #182 lists what stands between this and a live order.

## ADR-148 · Autopilot's live entry: behind a server switch that is off and allows the testnet only; live replaces paper for a mandate holder; an incomplete entry is left and its mandate paused (2026-11-27)
**Context.** Steps 1 to 3 and the first two parts of step 4 (ADR-146 the margin formula, ADR-147 the mandate) left Autopilot on paper. This is the first code that can send a real order without a person confirming it. The owner agreed the plan on 27 Nov 2026, with three choices put to them: live replaces paper for the trader, an incomplete entry is left and the mandate paused, and the loss limits count realised loss only.
**The switch.** `AUTOPILOT_LIVE` is `off` by default. `testnet` allows the live entry only while the trading host is, by exact name and over https, one of the exchange's named testnet hosts (`cdn-ind.testnet.deltaex.org`); against any other host (the real exchange, a proxy, an IP, a look-alike name) the configuration refuses to load. The label of ADR-092 calls every host that is not a known real one "testnet", which suits a warning label and not a permission, so this is an allow-list (found in the code review). The test harness's local mock host is allowed under NODE_ENV=test only. There is no value that allows production: that is a later decision, after deployment on fixed IPs and the owner's testnet margin check (GAPS #109, GAPS #183). With the switch off the engine is handed nothing and every line of the paper entry runs as before.
**Who is entered live.** A trader who holds an ACTIVE mandate with its exchange account, for a saved strategy that may trade live (on paper, approved, loss limited, no exit held on live, settings readable). For that trader and strategy the entry is live INSTEAD of paper: one trade at a time per strategy and coin, whichever it is (the one-open-trade index now covers live rows). Without such a mandate (none, paused, ended, account gone, approval withdrawn) the trader is entered on paper as always. The trade is priced again with the mandate's lots.
**The gate**, after the engine's own checks and in this order; each refusal is a log line that starts "Live:" and no order is sent, nor a paper trade taken, on that pass: the kill switches (operator, account); an entry at the mandate's lots; live trading in the plan and its monthly count; the trades open under the mandate; the loss limit for the day and for the week; the capital (this trade's margin estimate plus that of the mandate's open trades; no estimate is a refusal); the exchange's own word through the checks of every live placement (contract state, whole contracts, the placement caps, the wallet against the margin; a read that fails is a refusal); the exchange's mark of every leg within the mark band of the snapshot price; an order budget per account per minute.
**Loss limits.** Realised profit and loss of the mandate's own trades closed since 00:00 UTC of the day, and since 00:00 UTC of that week's Monday. A gain in the window is taken off a loss. The loss of a trade still open is NOT counted.
**The orders.** The strategy row is written as live first, in one transaction with its legs and its log line, with the approval read again inside it. A live leg is written with no entry price and no opening time: it is not held until the exchange fills it, which is what the rules engine, the settler and the books read as "never filled". Then one market order per leg, the bought options before the sold ones, each with its own client order id. The loop STOPS at the first leg that did not fill completely (refused, part filled, still open, unknown): unlike the manual placement, a sold option never goes out after a bought one failed. The exits are written only when every leg has filled, and the trade then gets its mark of completion (`strategies.autopilot_entry_complete_at`).
**An incomplete entry** is a live Autopilot trade without its mark of completion: a leg did not fill, an error came after the row was written, or the server stopped between the row and its orders or between the last fill and the arming of the exits (found by a sweep before each pass once the row is two minutes old). No order is retried and none is sent to close anything; no exit is armed; the legs the order loop provably never sent an order for (it stopped before reaching them, and they have no order row of any state) are taken off the trade without a price or a result, so that squaring it off by hand sends nothing for them; a leg that has an order row is never taken off, whatever the row says, because a refused, failed or unknown order can still turn out filled at the exchange (a gateway error after acceptance, a fill whose record was lost); a trade for which no order was ever sent is closed; where nobody can say what reached the exchange (an unexpected error, a server that stopped) nothing is taken off and nothing is closed, and the reason says to check the exchange; the mandate is paused with the reason; a log line and an audit line are written. What is held is loss-limited by its structure, because only loss-capped strategies can hold a mandate and their bought options went first. It waits for the trader to square it off (or it expires): until then the mandate cannot be resumed and enters nothing new.
